Cell decomposition and dual boundary complexes of character varieties
Tao Su

Abstract
. The weak geometric P=W conjecture of L. Katzarkov, A. Noll, P. Pandit, and C. Simpson asserts that for any smooth Betti moduli space ℳ B \mathcal{M}_{B} of complex dimension d d over a punctured Riemann surface, the dual boundary complex 𝔻 ​ ∂ ℳ B \mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{B} is homotopy equivalent to a ( d − 1 ) (d-1) -dimensional sphere. Here, we consider ℳ B \mathcal{M}_{B} as a generic G ​ L n ​ ( ℂ ) GL_{n}(\mathbb{C}) -character variety defined on a Riemann surface of genus g g , with local monodromies specified by generic semisimple conjugacy classes at k k punctures. In this article, we establish the weak geometric P=W conjecture for all very generic ℳ B \mathcal{M}_{B} in the sense that at least one conjugacy class is regular semisimple. A crucial step is to establish a stronger form of A. Mellit’s cell decomposition theorem, i.e. we decompose ℳ B \mathcal{M}_{B} (without passing to a vector bundle) into locally closed subvarieties of the form ( ℂ × ) d − 2 ​ b × 𝒜 (\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{d-2b}\times\mathcal{A} , where 𝒜 \mathcal{A} is stably isomorphic to ℂ b \mathbb{C}^{b} . A second ingredient involves a motivic characterization of the integral cohomology of dual boundary complexes developed in a subsequent article [ 59 ] . Following C. Simpson’s strategy, the proof is now an inductive computation of the dual boundary complexes from such a cell decomposition.



Introduction

Let
\Sigma
be a genus
g
closed Riemann surface with
k
punctures
\sigma=\{q_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},q_{k}\}
,
k\geq 1,2g+k\geq 3
, and
G=GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
Modulo extra input, the tame nonabelian Hodge correspondence over noncompact curves
[
51
,
34
]
induces a diffeomorphism

\mathrm{NAH}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{B}

between two moduli spaces: the Dolbeault moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}}
of stable filtered regular (parabolic)
G
-Higgs bundles on
(\Sigma,\sigma)
with parabolic degree
0
; and the Betti moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{B}
of stable filtered
G
-local systems on
\Sigma\setminus\sigma
with parabolic degree
0
.
For more on nonabelian Hodge theory, see
[
9
,
52
,
53
,
54
,
2
,
42
,
43
,
26
,
31
]
.

The geometric P=W conjecture of L. Katzarkov, A. Noll, P. Pandit and C. Simpson
[
32
,
55
]
predicts that, under
\mathrm{NAH}
, the “Hitchin fibration at infinity” of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}}
matches, up to homotopy, with a “fibration at infinity” intrinsic to the algebraic variety
\mathcal{M}_{B}
.
More concretely, on the Dolbeault side, the Hitchin fibration
h:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}
induces:

\overline{h}:N_{\mathrm{Dol}}^{*}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}}\setminus h^{-1}(B_{R}(0))\xrightarrow{h}\mathbb{A}\setminus B_{R}(0)\rightarrow(\mathbb{A}\setminus B_{R}(0))/\text{scaling}\penalty\ =S^{d-1},R\gg 0,d=\dim\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}};

On the Betti side, there is a fibration

\alpha:N_{B}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{B},

well-defined up to homotopy.
Here, we fix any log compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
of
\mathcal{M}_{B}
with simple normal crossing boundary divisor
\partial\mathcal{M}_{B}
.
Then,
N_{B}^{*}
is a punctured tubular neighborhood of
\partial\mathcal{M}_{B}
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
Moreover,
\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{B}
is the dual boundary complex of
\mathcal{M}_{B}
, i.e. the dual complex of the irreducible components of
\partial\mathcal{M}_{B}
.
Notice that the dual boundary complex is defined for any smooth quasi-projective variety, see Definition
3.2
.
For the moment, we skip the definition of
\alpha
. For the details, see Remark
3.9
.
Then

Conjecture 0.1

(
[
32
,
55
]
, Geometric P=W)
.

There is a homotopy commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathrm{Dol}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{B}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{d-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{NAH}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

As a weak form of the geometric P=W conjecture, we in particular have

Conjecture 0.2

(
[
32
,
55
]
, weak geometric P=W conjecture)
.

The dual boundary complex
\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{B}
is homotopy equivalent to the sphere
S^{d-1}
, where
d=\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
.

By a
folklore conjecture
, all (smooth) Betti moduli spaces (i.e. character varieties)
\mathcal{M}_{B}
are expected to be log Calabi-Yau (
CY
). This has been verified for the case
G=SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})

[
62
,
17
]
.
Then, the weak geometric P=W conjecture is closely related to the following
conjecture:

Conjecture 0.3

(Kontsevich(-Kollár-Xu))
.

The dual boundary complex of a log
CY
variety is (a finite quotient of) a sphere.

So far, the only known general result is due to J. Kollár and C. Xu
[
37
]
: If
X
is log Calabi-Yau of dimension
\leq 5
, then
\mathbb{D}\partial X
is a finite quotient of a sphere.

The geometric P=W conjecture was originally inspired by and aimed at a geometric interpretation of the (cohomological) P=W conjecture of M. de Cataldo, T. Hausel and L. Migliorini
[
12
]
.
The latter states that,
\mathrm{NAH}
exchanges the weight filtration (algebraic geometry) on
H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathbb{Q})
with the Perverse-Leray filtration (topology) on
H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}},\mathbb{Q})
:

\mathrm{NAH}^{*}(W_{2k}H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathbb{Q})=W_{2k+1}H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathbb{Q}))=P_{k}H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}},\mathbb{Q}).

After the results for rank
2

[
12
]
and respectively for genus
2

[
8
]
, the cohomological P=W conjecture has now been resolved independently by three groups
[
45
,
29
,
46
]
for the major case of twisted
GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
-character varieties (in particular,
k=1
).
See also
[
13
, Question 4.1.7]
,
[
11
, Conj.B]
,
[
39
,
19
]
,
[
41
, Conj.4.2.7]
, for the extensions of the P=W conjectures to the singular or stacky character varieties.

The geometric P=W conjecture is known to recover the cohomoloical P=W conjecture for the weight in top degree
[
41
, Thm.6.2.6]
.
In general, it’s not sufficient to imply the latter
[
41
, Rmk.6.2.11]
.
Nevertheless, the geometric P=W conjecture does contain some information beyond the cohomological P=W conjecture.
For example, the latter concerns only the cohomology with rational coefficients, hence captures only
H^{*}(\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathbb{Q})
via the identification (see e.g.
[
48
]
)

\widetilde{H}_{i-1}(\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathbb{Q})\cong\Gr_{2d}^{W}H^{2d-i}(\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathbb{Q}),\quad d=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}_{B}.

On the other hand, the former knows
\widetilde{H}^{*}(\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathbb{Z})
.

As explained above, to interpret the cohomogical P=W conjecture in all weights, a refinement of the geometric P=W conjecture is required.
Let’s make a complementary remark.
Assuming the folklore conjecture, we may consider only (dlt) log
CY
compactifications. Then we obtain a
refined dual boundary complex

\mathrm{DMR}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{B},\partial\mathcal{M}_{B})
,
well-defined up to PL-homeomorphism
[
14
, Prop.11]
.
By Conjecture
0.2
,
\mathrm{DMR}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{B},\partial\mathcal{M}_{B})
is a PL-sphere of dimension
d-1
.
It’s expected that
[
55
, §1.2]
this PL-sphere is closed related to the Kontsevich-Soibelman picture
[
36
]
: the Kontsevich-Soibelman chambers in the Hitchin base
\mathbb{A}
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}}
should correspond to the cells in
\mathrm{DMR}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{B},\partial\mathcal{M}_{B})
.

We haven’t said anything about the current state of the geometric P=W conjecture. Here we are.
The full geometric P=W conjecture is known for: the Painlevé cases
[
47
,
60
,
61
]
; the case
(g,k)=(1,0)
or
(k,n)=(0,1)

[
41
, Thm.B]
. Our major interest in this paper is its weak form, i.e. the weak geometric P=W conjecture
0.2
.
Previously, this is only known in a few cases:
G=SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})

[
33
,
55
,
17
]
; the Painlevé cases as above; singular character variety of any rank with
g=1
and
k=0

[
41
]
; smooth wild character variety of any rank with
g=0
and
k=1

[
58
]
.

Results

As a complement to the discussion above, our main result is the following:

Theorem 0.4

(Theorem
3.10
)
.

Let
(\Sigma,\sigma=\{q_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},q_{k}\})
be a
k
-punctured genus
g
Riemann surface, and
\mathcal{M}_{B}=\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
be its
G
-character variety of type

\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{k}
.
If
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is
very generic
, then the weak geometric P=W conjecture
0.2
holds for
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
.

Here,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is a
GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
-character variety on
\Sigma\setminus\sigma
with fixed semisimple conjugacy class
\mathcal{C}_{i}
around
q_{i}
;

\bm{\mu}=(\mu^{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\mu^{k})
, where
\mu^{i}=(\mu_{1}^{i}\geq\mu_{2}^{i}\geq\text{\tiny$\cdots$})\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
encodes the multiplicities of the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
. ‘
generic
’ means:

(\mathcal{C}_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\mathcal{C}_{k})
is
generic
in the sense of
[
27
, Def.2.1.1]
(Definition
1.1
); ‘
very generic
’ means:
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is in addition regular semisimple (Assumption
1.4
).

See Remark
3.16
for a discussion when
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is only generic.

To prove the main result, we need to improve A. Mellit’s cell decomposition
[
40
, §.7]
, which applies only to a vector bundle over very generic
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
.
More precisely, our second main result answers Mellit’s question in
[
40
, §1.4]
, i.e.
we give a honest cell decomposition for
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
:

Theorem 0.5

(Theorem
2.10
)
.

Any very generic
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
admits a cell decomposition:

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=\sqcup_{(\vec{w},p)\in\mathcal{W}^{*}}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p),

where each
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is a locally closed affine subvariety, such that

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p),\quad\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\times\mathbb{K}^{|U|}\cong\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)},

where
U\subset G
is the subgroup of unipotent upper triangular matrices,
\overline{b}(\vec{w},p):=b(\vec{w},p)-|U|
, and
\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)+2\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)=d_{\bm{\mu}}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is a
constant
.

Moreover, there exists a unique
(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})
such that
\dim\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})
is of maximal dimension
d_{\bm{\mu}}
. Equivalently,
\overline{a}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})=d_{\bm{\mu}}
(resp.
\overline{b}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})=0
).
In particular,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})
is an
open dense algebraic torus
:

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{d_{\bm{\mu}}},\quad\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})=\{\mathrm{pt}\}.

Note
:
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is stably isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)}
. We expect that,
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is in general not isomorphic to

\mathbb{A}^{\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)}
, hence gives a counterexample to the Zariski cancellation problem for dimension
b=\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)\geq 3
in characteristic zero.
See Remark
2.12
for a further discussion.

Similar to
[
40
, §.7]
, Theorem
0.5
is proved via a connection to braid varieties.
However, a more careful analysis is required. For the sake of clarity, we will give a self-contained proof. In fact, we use a somewhat different language (diagram calculus of matrices).
To prove Theorem
0.4
.(2) via Theorem
0.5
, the idea is to apply a
remove/reduction lemma
(Lemma
3.7
):
If
X
is a connected smooth quasi-projective variety, and
Z\subset X
is a smooth irreducible closed subvariety with nonempty open complement
U
, such that
\mathbb{D}\partial Z
is contractible, then we have a homotopy equivalence
\mathbb{D}\partial X\sim\mathbb{D}\partial U
.
We do can apply the remove lemma because of a key property
[
59
, Cor.0.3]
: if
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}
is stably isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{b}
for some
b\geq 1
, then
\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}
is contractible. This property is proved via a motivic characterization of the integral cohomology of dual boundary complexes (recalled in Proposition
3.6
).

As a final remark, we mention that the same strategy in this article can be applied to wild character varieties
[
4
,
6
]
.
This will be pursued elsewhere.

Organization

We have already explained the main ideas above. Now, we sketch the organization.

In Section
1
, we set up the basic notions related to character varieties. To clarify the computations in Section
2
, we introduce some diagram calculus of matrices in Section
1.3
.
Morally, it’s about braid matrix diagrams generalizing braid matrices associated to positive braids. In Section
1.4
, we review braid varieties, complemented by Appendix
A
.

In Section
2
, we prove a strong form of the cell decomposition for
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
(Theorem
2.10
). This involves some routine diagram calculus in Sections
2.1
-
2.2
.
In Sections
2.3
-
2.4
, we have borrowed statements on quotients of varieties from Appendix
B
.
In Section
2.5
, we illustrate Theorem
2.10
by two examples.

In Section
3
, we study dual boundary complexes of character varieties.
Section
3.1
reviews the basics on dual boundary complexes.
In Section
3.2
, we treat the weak geometric P=W conjecture (Theorem
3.10
).
To end this article, we add a few remarks on some further directions in Section
3.3
.

1.
Setup

Fix the base field
\mathbb{K}
to be algebraically closed of characteristic
0
. For simplicity,
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
.
A
\mathbb{K}
-variety
means a
reduced separated scheme of finite type
over
\mathbb{K}
.

Convention

1
: Let
H
be a linear algebraic group acting on a variety
X
over
\mathbb{K}
, then

•

A principal
H
-bundle (or a fiber bundle) means so in the étale topology, unless stated otherwise.

•

For
H
reductive and
X
affine,
X//H=\mathrm{Spec}\penalty\ \mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{H}
denotes the affine GIT quotient.

•

[X/H]
denotes the quotient stack of
X
by
H
.

•

If
H
acts freely on
X
, and
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is principal
H
-bundle over a
\mathbb{K}
-variety, so also a geometric quotient (see
[
30
, Def.3.27]
, Proposition
B.9
). Then denote
X/H:=Y
.

Equivalently, this says that the quotient stack
[X/H]
is representable by
Y
. Thus, we may also use the identification
[X/H]=X/H
.

We refer to Appendix
B
for more background on various quotients of varieties.

1.1.
Generic character varieties

Recall that,
(\Sigma,\sigma=\{q_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},q_{k}\})
is a
k
-punctured genus
g
Riemann surface,
k\geq 1,2g+k\geq 3
.
Let
T\subset G=GL_{n}(\mathbb{K})
be the diagonal maximal torus.
Let
(C_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},C_{k})\in T^{k}
be semisimple elements of type
\bm{\mu}:=(\mu^{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\mu^{k})\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{k}
.
That is, the multiplicities of eigenvalues of
C_{i}
define a partition of
n
:
\mu^{i}=(\mu_{1}^{i},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\mu_{r_{i}}^{i})\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=\mathcal{M}_{B}=\mathcal{M}_{B}(\Sigma,\sigma,G;C_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},C_{k})

be the character variety of
G
-local systems on
\Sigma
whose local monodromy around
q_{i}
is conjugate to
C_{i}
.
More precisely, define the affine
\mathbb{K}
-variety

M_{B}=M_{B}(\Sigma,\sigma,G;C_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},C_{k}):=\{(A_{j})_{j=1}^{2g},x_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},x_{k})\in G^{2g+k}:\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}A_{2j-1},A_{2j}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k}$}x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}=\mathsf{id}\},

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}-,-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}
stands for the multiplicative commutator. It has an action of the reductive group

G_{\mathrm{par}}:=G\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k}$}Z(C_{i}),\quad Z(C_{i})=\text{ the centralizer of }C_{i},

with the action given by:

(h_{0},h_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},h_{k})\cdot(A_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},A_{2j},x_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},x_{k}):=(h_{0}A_{1}h_{0}^{-1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},h_{0}A_{2j}h_{0}^{-1},h_{0}x_{1}h_{1}^{-1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},h_{0}x_{k}h_{k}^{-1}).

Then, the diagonal
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
acts trivially on
M_{B}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}:=M_{B}//G_{\mathrm{par}}
is the affine GIT quotient.

Definition 1.1

(
[
40
, Def.4.6.1]
)
.

(C_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},C_{k})\in T^{k}
is
generic
if:

\prod_{i=1}^{k}\det C_{i}=1
, and for any
1\leq n^{\prime}<n
, take any
n^{\prime}
eigenvalues
\alpha_{i,1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\alpha_{i,n^{\prime}}
of each
C_{i}
, have

\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}$}\alpha_{i,j}\neq 1.

In this case,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is called a
generic character variety
.

Lemma 1.2

(
[
27
, Thm.2.1.5]
,
[
28
, Thm.5.1.1]
)
.

If
(C_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},C_{k})
is generic of type
\bm{\mu}
, then
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=M_{B}/PG_{\mathrm{par}}
(if nonempty) is a connected smooth affine
\mathbb{K}
-variety of dimension

(1.1.1)

d_{\bm{\mu}}:=n^{2}(2g-2+k)-\sum_{i,j}(\mu_{j}^{i})^{2}+2,

and the quotient map
\pi:M_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=M_{B}/PG_{\mathrm{par}}
is a principal
PG_{\mathrm{par}}
-bundle.

Definition 1.3
.

C_{i}\in T
is
ordered nicely
if it’s of the form

\diag(\lambda_{i,1}I_{\mu_{1}^{i}},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\lambda_{i,r_{i}}I_{\mu_{r_{i}}^{i}})
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that each
C_{i}\in T
is
ordered nicely
,
so that
Z(C_{i})\subset G
is the Levi subgroup of block-diagonal matrices of type
\mu^{i}
.

1.2.
Very generic character varieties

Let
B\subset G
be the standard Borel subgroup of upper triangular elements, with unipotent radical
U\subset B
.
We make the
very generic
assumption:

Assumption 1.4
.

C_{k}
is regular semisimple. So,
Z(C_{k})=T
and
\mu^{k}=(1^{n})\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

Then,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is called a
very generic character variety
.
The nice feature in this case is a cell decomposition
[
40
]
, and an enhanced version will be proved in Theorem
2.10
.

The first step is as follows:
Taking the diagonal gives the quotient morphism

D:B\twoheadrightarrow B/U\cong T.

Define a closed (affine) subvariety of
M_{B}
by

(1.2.1)

M_{B}^{\prime}:=M_{B}\cap(G^{2g+k-1}\times U),

and a closed subgroup of
G_{\mathrm{par}}
by

(1.2.2)

B_{\mathrm{par}}=B\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}Z(C_{i})\hookrightarrow G_{\mathrm{par}}:(b,h_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},h_{k-1})\mapsto(b,h_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},h_{k-1},D(b)).

Denote
PB_{\mathrm{par}}:=B_{\mathrm{par}}/\mathbb{K}^{\times}
.
We have mutually inverse isomorphisms of
G_{\mathrm{par}}
-varieties

\displaystyle PG_{\mathrm{par}}\times^{PB_{\mathrm{par}}}M_{B}^{\prime}:=(PG_{\mathrm{par}}\times M_{B}^{\prime})/PB_{\mathrm{par}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}M_{B}:[g_{\mathrm{par}},m^{\prime}_{B}=(A_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},x_{k})]\mapsto g_{\mathrm{par}}\cdot m_{B}^{\prime},

\displaystyle M_{B}\rightarrow PG_{\mathrm{par}}\times^{PB_{\mathrm{par}}}M_{B}^{\prime}:(A_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},x_{k})\mapsto[(x_{k},\mathsf{id},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\mathsf{id}),((x_{k}^{-1}A_{j}x_{k})_{j=1}^{2g},(x_{k}^{-1}x_{i})_{i=1}^{k})],

where
PG_{\mathrm{par}}\times^{PB_{\mathrm{par}}}M_{B}^{\prime}
is well-defined by Proposition
B.9
.
Then by Proposition
B.12
and Proposition
B.9
, we obtain a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties:

M_{B}^{\prime}/PB_{\mathrm{par}}\cong(PG_{\mathrm{par}}\times^{PB_{\mathrm{par}}}M_{B}^{\prime})/PG_{\mathrm{par}}\cong M_{B}/PG_{\mathrm{par}}=\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}},

and the quotient
\pi^{\prime}:M_{B}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=M_{B}^{\prime}/PB_{\mathrm{par}}
is a principal
PB_{\mathrm{par}}
-bundle.
Observe that we have a quotient group
PB_{\mathrm{par}}/U\cong PT_{\mathrm{par}}:=(T\times\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}Z(C_{i}))/\mathbb{K}^{\times}
.

Take the coordinate change

(1.2.3)

U\rightarrow U:x_{k}\mapsto u_{k}:=x_{k}C_{k}x_{k}^{-1}C_{k}^{-1}.

We can re-write

(1.2.4)

M_{B}^{\prime}=\{(A_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},x_{k-1},u_{k})\in G^{2g+k-1}\times U:(\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}A_{2j-1},A_{2j}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}})(\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1})u_{k}C_{k}=\mathsf{id}\}

with the action of
B_{\mathrm{par}}
given by:

(1.2.5)

(b,h_{1},\text{\tiny$\text{\tiny$\cdots$}$},h_{k-1})\cdot(A_{1},\text{\tiny$\text{\tiny$\cdots$}$},x_{k-1},u_{k}):=(bA_{1}b^{-1},\text{\tiny$\text{\tiny$\cdots$}$},bx_{k-1}h_{k-1}^{-1},bu_{k}(b^{C_{k}})^{-1}),\penalty\ \penalty\ b^{C_{k}}:=C_{k}bC_{k}^{-1}.

Next, for any
nicely ordered

C\in T
, take the parabolic subgroup
P=BZ(C)\subset G
. Denote the Weyl groups
W\cong S_{n}\subset G
,
W(C):=W(Z(C))
.
Recall the Bruhat cell decomposition

G=\sqcup_{w\in W/W(C)}B\dot{w}P,

where
\dot{w}\in W
denotes the
shortest
representative of
w\in W/W(C)
.

In our setting,
P_{i}:=BZ(C_{i})\subset G
.
For each sequence

(1.2.6)

\vec{w}=(\tau_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\tau_{2g},w_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},w_{k-1})\in W^{2g}\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}W/W(C_{i}),

we obtain a locally closed affine
B_{\mathrm{par}}
-subvariety of
M_{B}^{\prime}
:

(1.2.7)

M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w}):=M_{B}^{\prime}\cap(\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{2g}$}B\tau_{j}B\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}B\dot{w}_{i}P_{i}\times U)=M_{B}\cap(\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{2g}$}B\tau_{j}B\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}B\dot{w}_{i}P_{i}\times U).

Define

(1.2.8)

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}):=\pi^{\prime}(M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w}))\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=M_{B}^{\prime}/PB_{\mathrm{par}}.

By Corollary
B.11
,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is a locally closed
\mathbb{K}
-subvariety, and the quotient map

\pi_{\vec{w}}^{\prime}:=\pi^{\prime}|_{M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})}:M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})=M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})/PB_{\mathrm{par}}

is a principal
PB_{\mathrm{par}}
bundle.

To obtain a cell decomposition for
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
, the idea is to decompose
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
further, via a connection to the so-called
braid varieties
.
We will come back to this point after some preparations.

1.3.
Diagram calculus of matrices

To relate character and braid varieties, we introduce some diagram calculus of matrices.
Denote

\displaystyle\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}:=\langle\sigma_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\sigma_{n-1}\rangle;\penalty\ \penalty\ (\text{free monoid of $n$-strand (positive) braid presentations})

\displaystyle\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}:=\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}/(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1},\forall i;\penalty\ \sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}=\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i},\forall|i-j|>1);\penalty\ \penalty\ (\text{$n$-strand positive braids})

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}:\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}\rightarrow\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}/(\sigma_{i}^{2}=1,\forall i)\cong S_{n}:\sigma_{k}\mapsto\mathrm{s}_{k}:=\mathrm{s}(\sigma_{k})=(k\penalty\ k+1).\penalty\ \penalty\ (\text{underlying permuations})

Convention

:

\beta_{1}

\beta_{2}

1,2,\text{\tiny$\cdots$},n
. Denote

Definition 1.5
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon):=I_{n}+\epsilon e_{i,j}\in G,\penalty\ 1\leq i<j\leq n,\epsilon\in\mathbb{K};\quad[\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon)]:=\text{ Figure \ref{fig:elementary_braid_matrix_diagrams} (middle)}.

Definition 1.6

(Braid matrix diagram presentations)
.

(1)

The monoid of
braid matrix diagram presentations
(
\mathbf{bmdp}
) of rank
n
over
\mathbb{K}
is:

\mathrm{FBD}_{n}:=\langle\sigma_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\sigma_{n-1},[\mathrm{K}_{k}(\epsilon)],[\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon^{\prime})]\rangle/([\mathrm{K}_{k}(1)]=\mathsf{id}_{n}=[\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(0)]),\quad\Gamma_{2}\circ\Gamma_{1}:=[\Gamma_{1}|\Gamma_{2}].

So, a
\mathbf{bmdp}
can be viewd as a finite concatenation of elementary braid matrix diagrams.

(2)

Define a morphism of monoids

g_{-}:\mathrm{FBD}_{n}\rightarrow G:\sigma_{k}\mapsto\mathrm{s}_{k},\penalty\ \penalty\ [\mathrm{K}_{k}(\epsilon)]\mapsto\mathrm{K}_{k}(\epsilon),\penalty\ \penalty\ [\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon^{\prime})]\mapsto\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon^{\prime}).

Two
\mathbf{bmdp}
’s
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\in\mathrm{FBD}_{n}
are
weakly equivalent
if
g_{\Gamma_{1}}=g_{\Gamma_{2}}
, denoted as
\Gamma_{1}\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{w}$}}{\sim}\Gamma_{2}
.

Lemma 1.7

(
Elementary moves
of
\mathbf{bmdp}
’s)
.

Denote
i^{\prime}:=\mathrm{s}_{k}(i)
, then

\mathrm{K}_{k}(\epsilon_{1})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{K}_{\ell}(\epsilon_{2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{K}_{k}(\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2})&\text{$k=\ell$},\\
\mathrm{K}_{\ell}(\epsilon_{2})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{K}_{k}(\epsilon_{1})&\text{$k\neq\ell$}.\end{array}\right.

\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{1})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{K}_{k}(\epsilon_{2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{K}_{i}(\epsilon_{2})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{2}^{-1}\epsilon_{1})&\text{$k=i$},\\
\mathrm{K}_{i}(\epsilon_{2})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2})&\text{$k=j$},\\
\mathrm{K}_{i}(\epsilon_{2})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{1})&\text{$k\neq i,j$}.\end{array}\right.

\left.\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{s}_{k}\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{K}_{i}(\epsilon)=\mathrm{K}_{i^{\prime}}(\epsilon)\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{s}_{k};\\
\mathrm{s}_{k}\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon)=\mathrm{H}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(\epsilon)\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{s}_{k}.\end{array}\right.

\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{1})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{H}_{k,\ell}(\epsilon_{2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{1}\text{\tiny$+$}\epsilon_{2})&\text{$(k,\ell)=(i,j)$},\\
\mathrm{H}_{j,\ell}(\epsilon_{2})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{H}_{i,\ell}(\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{1})&\text{$k=j$},\\
\mathrm{H}_{k,i}(\epsilon_{2})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{H}_{k,j}(\text{\tiny$-$}\epsilon_{2}\epsilon_{1})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{1})&\text{$\ell=i$},\\
\mathrm{H}_{k,\ell}(\epsilon_{2})\text{\tiny$\circ$}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{1})&\text{else}.\end{array}\right.

Each identity is either trivial (commutative), or represented by an (
elementary
) move in Figure
1.3.3
.

Proof.

Let
e_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},e_{n}
be the
standard basis
for
\mathbb{K}^{n}
. It’s direct to check that, both sides of each identity applied to every
e_{i}
, give the same results. See also Figure
1.3.3
for an illustration.
∎

Definition 1.8

(Braid matrix diagrams)
.

(1)

Let
\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
be the quotient of
\mathrm{FBD}_{n}
by elementary moves. Then
\exists
a morphism of monoids

\beta_{-}:\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}:\sigma_{k}\mapsto\sigma_{k},\penalty\ \penalty\ [\mathrm{K}_{k}(\epsilon)]\mapsto\mathsf{id}_{n},\penalty\ \penalty\ [\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon^{\prime})]\mapsto\mathsf{id}_{n}.

(2)

The monoid
\mathrm{BD}_{n}
of rank
n

braid matrix diagrams
(
\mathbf{bmd}
) is the quotient of
\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
by usual
braid relations/moves
.
So,
\mathrm{BD}_{n}=\mathrm{FBD}_{n}/\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{b}$}}{\sim}
, with
\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{b}$}}{\sim}
1
1

1

Term
\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{b}$}}{\sim}
as
braid equivalence
. Clearly,
\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{b}$}}{\sim}
implies
\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{w}$}}{\sim}
. On the other hand,
\sigma_{k}^{2}\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{w}$}}{\sim}\mathsf{id}_{n}
, but
\sigma_{k}^{2}\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{b}$}}{\nsim}\mathsf{id}_{n}
.
generated by elementary and braid moves.

(3)

\beta_{-}:\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}
and
g_{-}:\mathrm{FBD}_{n}\rightarrow G
induce morphisms of monoids:

\beta_{-}:\mathrm{BD}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+},\quad g_{-}:\mathrm{BD}_{n}\rightarrow G.

Remark 1.9
.

We have natural morphisms of monoids

i:\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}:\sigma_{k}\mapsto\sigma_{k},\quad\rightsquigarrow\quad i:\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}\rightarrow\mathrm{BD}_{n}:\sigma_{k}\mapsto\sigma_{k}.

\beta_{-}\circ i=\mathsf{id}
, so
i
induces embeddings
\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}\hookrightarrow\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
and
\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{BD}_{n}
. This morally explains the terminology.
Altogether, we get a commutative diagram of monoids:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{FBD}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{BD}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{-}}

Next,
\mathrm{s}:\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}\rightarrow S_{n}
admits a
canonical section
(as a map of sets) characterized by:

(1.3.1)

[-]:S_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}\subset\mathrm{BD}_{n}:w\mapsto[w],\text{ with }\mathrm{s}([w])=w,\ell([w])=\ell(w).

As we have seen,
[\mathrm{s}_{k}]=\sigma_{k}
. Up to a choice, we may assume
[w]\in\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}
. By definition,

(1.3.2)

[w_{1}]\circ[w_{2}]=[w_{1}w_{2}]\in\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}\Leftrightarrow\ell(w_{1}w_{2})=\ell(w_{1})+\ell(w_{2}).

Also, for
B\subset G
, there is a canonical
morphism of monoids
extending Definition
1.5
:

(1.3.3)

[-]:B\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}:b\mapsto[b]\text{ with }g_{[b]}=b.

Proposition 1.10
.

The map
g_{-}:\mathrm{BD}_{n}\rightarrow G
has a
canonical section
(as a map of sets)

[-]:G=\sqcup_{w\in W}BwB\rightarrow\mathrm{BD}_{n}:x=b_{1}wb_{2}\mapsto[x]:=[b_{1}]\circ[w]\circ[b_{2}].

Up to a choice
[w]\in\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}
,
[x]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
. Moreover, for any other
x^{\prime}=b_{1}^{\prime}w^{\prime}b_{2}^{\prime}\in Bw^{\prime}B
,

[x]\circ[x^{\prime}]=[xx^{\prime}]\in\mathrm{BD}_{n}\Leftrightarrow\ell(ww^{\prime})=\ell(w)+\ell(w^{\prime})\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{inv}(ww^{\prime})=w^{\prime-1}(\mathrm{inv}(w))\sqcup\mathrm{inv}(w^{\prime}).

In this case, we obtain
unique decompositions
for
xx^{\prime}\in Bww^{\prime}B=Bww^{\prime}U_{ww^{\prime}}^{-}
:

(1.3.4)

Bww^{\prime}U_{ww^{\prime}}^{-}=BwU_{w}^{-}w^{\prime}U_{w^{\prime}}^{-}=U_{w^{-1}}^{-}wBw^{\prime}U_{w^{\prime}}^{-}=U_{w^{-1}}^{-}wU_{w^{\prime-1}}^{-}w^{\prime}B=U_{w^{\prime-1}w^{-1}}^{-}ww^{\prime}B.

Convention

3
: Often, for
[A]\in\mathrm{BD}_{n}
, we use the same notation for its lift in
\mathrm{FBD}_{n}
or
\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
.

Before giving the proof, we discuss some linear algebra.
Denote

\mathcal{I}:=\{(i,j):1\leq i<j\leq n\}.

Then
U=I_{n}+\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{K}e_{i,j}\subset B\subset G
.
We say a subset
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{I}
is
multiplicative
, if

(i,j),(j,k)\in\mathcal{J}\Rightarrow(i,k)\in\mathcal{J}.

For each
m\geq 1
, denote
\mathcal{J}_{1}:=\mathcal{J}
and in general:

\mathcal{J}_{m}:=\{(j_{0},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},j_{m}):(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\mathcal{J},\forall 0\leq k\leq m-1\}.

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{I}
be multiplicative. Denote

U_{\mathcal{J}}:=I_{n}+\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{K}e_{i,j}\subset U
.
Then

(1)

U_{\mathcal{J}}\subset U
is a closed subgroup.

(2)

Any fixed total order
\preceq
on
\mathcal{J}
induces an isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties

\phi_{\preceq}:\mathbb{A}^{|\mathcal{J}|}\rightarrow U_{\mathcal{J}}:(\epsilon_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}}\mapsto\text{\tiny$\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{J}}$}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{i,j})

Proof.

We prove
(2)
.
(1)
is similar. Say,
\mathcal{J}=\{(i_{1},j_{1})<\text{\tiny$\cdots$}<(i_{N},j_{N})\}
. Then

\text{\tiny$\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{J}}$}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{i,j})=\text{\tiny$\prod_{\ell=1}^{N}$}(I_{n}+\epsilon_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}e_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}})=I_{n}+\text{\tiny$\sum_{m\geq 1}\sum_{(k_{0},k_{1})<\text{\tiny$\cdots$}<(k_{m-1},k_{m})\text{ in $\mathcal{J}$}}$}\epsilon_{k_{0},k_{1}}\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\epsilon_{k_{m-1},k_{m}}e_{k_{0},k_{m}}.

The equation
I_{n}+\text{\tiny$\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{J}}$}a_{ij}e_{i,j}=\text{\tiny$\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{J}}$}\mathrm{H}_{i,j}(\epsilon_{i,j})
becomes

a_{i,j}=\epsilon_{i,j}+\text{\tiny$\sum_{m\geq 2}\sum_{(i,k_{1})<\text{\tiny$\cdots$}<(k_{m-1},j)\text{ in $\mathcal{J}$}}$}\epsilon_{i,k_{1}}\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\epsilon_{k_{m-1},j},\quad\forall(i,j)\in\mathcal{J}.

This uniquely determines the
\epsilon_{i,j}
’s inductively, in the increasing (partial) order on
|j-i|
.
∎

For any permuation
w\in W=S_{n}
, denote

\mathrm{inv}(w):=\{(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}:i<j,w(i)>w(j)\};\quad\mathrm{noinv}(w):=\{(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}:i<j,w(i)<w(j)\}.

Then
\ell(w)=|\mathrm{inv}(w)|
, and
\mathrm{inv}(w)
,
\mathrm{noinv}(w)
are multiplicative subsets of
\mathcal{I}
.
So,

U_{w}^{+}:=U_{\mathrm{noinv}(w)}=\mathsf{id}+\text{\tiny$\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathrm{noinv}(w)}$}\mathbb{K}e_{i,j};\quad U_{w}^{-}:=U_{\mathrm{inv}(w)}=\mathsf{id}+\text{\tiny$\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathrm{inv}(w)}$}\mathbb{K}e_{i,j},

are closed subgroups of
U
, to which Lemma
1.11
apply.
Alternatively, we have

(1.3.5)

U_{w}^{+}=U\cap w^{-1}Uw=w^{-1}U_{w^{-1}}^{+}w,\quad U_{w}^{-}=U\cap w^{-1}U^{-}w,

with
U^{-}\subset G
the opposite unipotent subgroup.
By Lemma
1.11
(2), we have decompositions:

(1.3.6)

U=U_{w}^{+}U_{w}^{-}=U_{w}^{-}U_{w}^{+};\quad BwB=U_{w^{-1}}^{-}wB=BwU_{w}^{-}.

Proof of Proposition
1.10
.

Clearly,
[-]
is well-defined,
g_{-}\circ[-]=\mathsf{id}
.
To show equivalences.

“
\text{LHS}\Rightarrow\text{Middle}
”: Say,
xx^{\prime}\in B\widetilde{w}B
,
\widetilde{w}\in S_{n}
, so
\beta_{[xx^{\prime}]}=[\widetilde{w}]
.
If
[x]\circ[x^{\prime}]=[xx^{\prime}]
, then

[\widetilde{w}]=\beta_{[xx^{\prime}]}=\beta_{[x]}\circ\beta_{[x^{\prime}]}=[w]\circ[w^{\prime}],\quad\widetilde{w}=\mathrm{s}(\beta_{[xx^{\prime}]})=\mathrm{s}(\beta_{[x]})\circ\mathrm{s}(\beta_{[x^{\prime}]})=ww^{\prime}.

Thus,

\ell(ww^{\prime})=\ell([ww^{\prime}])=\ell([w]\circ[w^{\prime}])=\ell([w])+\ell([w^{\prime}])=\ell(w)+\ell(w^{\prime})
, as desired.

“
\text{Middle}\Rightarrow\text{RHS}
”:
Denote

\displaystyle(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}):=(w^{\prime}(a),w^{\prime}(b)),\quad(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}):=(w(a^{\prime}),w(b^{\prime})),\quad 1\leq a<b\leq n;

\displaystyle I_{+-}:=\{(a,b)\in\mathcal{I}:a^{\prime}<b^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}>b^{\prime\prime}\};\quad I_{-+}:=\{(a,b)\in\mathcal{I}:a^{\prime}>b^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}>b^{\prime\prime}\}.

Observe that
\mathrm{inv}(ww^{\prime})=I_{+-}\sqcup I_{-+}
and
w^{\prime}
induces bijections

\displaystyle w^{\prime}:I_{+-}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{noinv}(w^{\prime-1})\cap\mathrm{inv}(w);\penalty\ \penalty\ R\circ w^{\prime}:I_{-+}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{inv}(w^{\prime-1})\cap\mathrm{noinv}(w),\penalty\ \penalty\ R(i,j):=(j,i);

\displaystyle w^{\prime}\sqcup R\circ w^{\prime}:\mathrm{inv}(ww^{\prime})=I_{+-}\sqcup I_{-+}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{noinv}(w^{\prime-1})\cap\mathrm{inv}(w)\sqcup\mathrm{inv}(w^{\prime-1})\cap\mathrm{noinv}(w).

Hence,

\ell(ww^{\prime})=|\mathrm{noinv}(w^{\prime-1})\cap\mathrm{inv}(w)|+|\mathrm{inv}(w^{\prime-1})\cap\mathrm{noinv}(w)|\leq|\mathrm{inv}(w)|+|\mathrm{inv}(w^{\prime-1})|=\ell(w)+\ell(w^{\prime}),

with equality holds if and only if
\mathrm{inv}(w)\cap\mathrm{inv}(w^{\prime-1})=\varnothing
.
Then,
I_{+-}=w^{\prime-1}(\mathrm{inv}(w))
, and
I_{-+}=(R\circ w^{\prime})^{-1}(\mathrm{inv}(w^{\prime-1}))=\mathrm{inv}(w^{\prime})
.
Hence,

\mathrm{inv}(ww^{\prime})=w^{\prime-1}(\mathrm{inv}(w))\sqcup\mathrm{inv}(w^{\prime})
,
as desired.

“
\text{RHS}\Rightarrow\text{Middle}
” is clear, so
\text{RHS}\Leftrightarrow\text{Middle}
.

“
\text{Middle}\Rightarrow\text{LHS}
”:
By above,
\mathrm{noinv}(w)\cup\mathrm{noinv}(w^{\prime-1})=\mathcal{I}
.
By Lemma
1.11
, we get a surjection

m:U_{w}^{+}\times U_{w^{\prime-1}}^{+}\rightarrow U:(u_{1},u_{2})\mapsto u_{1}u_{2}.

By (
1.3.6
), can assume
b_{2}\in U_{w}^{-}
,
b_{1}^{\prime}\in U_{w^{\prime-1}}^{-}
. By above, we may write

b_{2}b_{1}^{\prime}=u_{1}u_{2},\penalty\ u_{1}\in U_{w}^{+},u_{2}\in U_{w^{\prime-1}}^{+}
.
So,
xx^{\prime}=(b_{1}wu_{1}w^{-1})ww^{\prime}(w^{\prime-1}u_{2}w^{\prime}b_{2}^{\prime})\in Bww^{\prime}B
, and

\displaystyle[xx^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[b_{1}wu_{1}w^{-1}]\circ[ww^{\prime}]\circ[w^{\prime-1}u_{2}w^{\prime}b_{2}^{\prime}]\in\mathrm{BD}_{n}\quad(\text{by definition})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[b_{1}]\circ[wu_{1}w^{-1}]\circ[w]\circ[w^{\prime}]\circ[w^{\prime-1}u_{2}w^{\prime}]\circ[b_{2}^{\prime}]\quad(\text{by (\ref{eqn:composition_of_positive_braids_lifting_permutations}), (\ref{eqn:braid_matrix_diagrams_lifting_Borel_elements})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[b_{1}]\circ([w]\circ[u_{1}])\circ([u_{2}]\circ[w^{\prime}])\circ[b_{2}^{\prime}]\quad(\text{by elementary moves as in Figure \ref{fig:elementary_moves}}.(8))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[b_{1}]\circ[w]\circ[b_{2}]\circ[b_{1}^{\prime}]\circ[w^{\prime}]\circ[b_{2}^{\prime}]=[x]\circ[x^{\prime}].\quad(\text{by (\ref{eqn:braid_matrix_diagrams_lifting_Borel_elements})})

It remains to show the decompositions. By Lemma
1.11
, we get an isomorphism

m:U_{w^{\prime-1}(\mathrm{inv}(w))}\times U_{\mathrm{inv}(w^{\prime})}=w^{\prime-1}U_{w}^{-}w^{\prime}\times U_{w^{\prime}}^{-}\rightarrow U_{\mathrm{inv}(ww^{\prime})}=U_{ww^{\prime}}^{-}:(u_{1},u_{2})\mapsto u_{1}u_{2}

So is
m:U_{w^{\prime}}^{-}\times w^{\prime-1}U_{w}^{-}w^{\prime}\rightarrow U_{ww^{\prime}}^{-}
. Then,
U_{ww^{\prime}}^{-}=(w^{\prime-1}U_{w}^{-}w^{\prime})U_{w^{\prime}}^{-}=U_{w^{\prime}}^{-}(w^{\prime-1}U_{w}^{-}w^{\prime})
.
Also, the same result applies to
(ww^{\prime})^{-1}=w^{\prime-1}w^{-1}
.
Altogether, we get unique decompositions

Bww^{\prime}B=Bww^{\prime}U_{ww^{\prime}}^{-}=BwU_{w}^{-}w^{\prime}U_{w^{\prime}}^{-}=U_{w^{-1}}^{-}wBw^{\prime}U_{w^{\prime}}^{-}=U_{w^{-1}}^{-}wU_{w^{\prime-1}}^{-}w^{\prime}B=U_{w^{\prime-1}w^{-1}}^{-}ww^{\prime}B.

∎

Back to character varieties.
Let’s reinterpret
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
(see (
1.2.7
)) via braid matrix diagrams.

First, we set up some notations. We have seen (unique) decompositions:

(1.3.7)

\displaystyle U=U_{w}^{+}U_{w}^{-}:u=L_{w}^{+}(u)L_{w}^{-}(u),\quad B=TU=(TU_{w}^{+})U_{w}^{-}:b=D(b)b_{R}=L_{w}^{+}(b)L_{w}^{-}(b);

\displaystyle U=U_{w}^{-}U_{w}^{+}:u=R_{w}^{-}(u)R_{w}^{+}(u),\quad B=UT=(U_{w}^{-})(U_{w}^{+}T):b=b_{L}D(b)=R_{w}^{-}(b)R_{w}^{+}(b).

Similarly, each parabolic
P_{i}\subset G
has decompositions

(1.3.8)

P_{i}=N_{i}Z(C_{i})=Z(C_{i})N_{i}:p_{i}=L_{i}(p_{i})D_{i}(p_{i})=D_{i}(p_{i})R_{i}(p_{i}),\quad N_{i}:=P_{i}\cap U.

As the shortest representative of
w_{i}\in W/W(C_{i})
,
\dot{w}_{i}
gives

U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}\subset N_{i},\quad Z(C_{i})\cap U\subset U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}.

Thus, by Lemma
1.11
, we obtain decompositions

(1.3.9)

N_{i}=U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})=(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-};\penalty\ \penalty\ U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}=(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})(Z(C_{i})\cap U)=(Z(C_{i})\cap U)(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i}).

Now, we reinterpret
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
. To begin with, we have a canonical isomorphism

(1.3.10)

U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}\times N_{i}\times Z(C_{i})\cong U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}\dot{w}_{i}P_{i}=B\dot{w}_{i}P_{i}:(\nu_{i},n_{i},z_{i})\mapsto x_{i}=\nu_{i}\dot{w}_{i}n_{i}z_{i}.

Then,
x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}=\nu_{i}\dot{w}_{i}n_{i}C_{i}n_{i}^{-1}\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}\nu_{i}^{-1}=\nu_{i}\dot{w}_{i}n_{i}^{\prime}C_{i}\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}\nu_{i}^{-1}
. Here, we use the isomorphism

(1.3.11)

N_{i}\xrightarrow{\cong}N_{i}:n_{i}\mapsto n_{i}^{\prime}=n_{i}C_{i}n_{i}^{-1}C_{i}^{-1}.

Recall that for
b\in B
,
[b]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
(see (
1.3.3
)),
\beta_{[b]}=\mathsf{id}_{n}\in\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}
and
g_{[b]}=b
.
Define

(1.3.12)

[x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}]^{\prime}:=[\nu_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[n_{i}^{\prime}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[\nu_{i}^{-1}]=[\nu_{i}^{-1}|\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}|C_{i}|n_{i}^{\prime}|\dot{w}_{i}|\nu_{i}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

Recall that
A_{j}\in B\tau_{j}B
, with Proposition
1.10
in mind, define
[\mathrm{M}_{\vec{w}}]=[\mathrm{M}_{\vec{w}}((A_{j})_{j},(x_{i})_{i},u_{k})]
by

(1.3.13)

[\mathrm{M}_{\vec{w}}]:=\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}([A_{2j-1}]\circ[A_{2j}]\circ[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[A_{2j}^{-1}])\circ\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}([x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}]^{\prime})\circ[u_{k}C_{k}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

Then by (
1.2.7
) and (

), the defining equation for

reads

(1.3.14)

[\mathrm{M}_{\vec{w}}]=[\mathrm{M}_{\vec{w}}((A_{j})_{j=1}^{2g},(x_{i})_{i=1}^{k-1},u_{k})]\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{w}$}}{\sim}\mathsf{id}_{n}\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n},

where

is a
\mathbf{bmdp}
(with varying coefficients) but with fixed

(1.3.15)

The next idea is to canonicalize
[\mathrm{M}_{\vec{w}}]
by diagram calculus: push every handleslide or scaling in

via elementary moves,
to the right as far as possible. Later on, we’ll see this leads to braid varieties.

Definition 1.14

(Braid varieties)
.

Let
\beta=\sigma_{i_{\ell}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{1}}\in\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}
. For each
1\leq j\leq\ell
, denote

(1.4.3)

f_{j}:\mathbb{A}^{j}\rightarrow G:(\epsilon_{j},\cdots,\epsilon_{1})\mapsto\mathrm{B}_{i_{j}}(\epsilon_{j})\cdots\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1}).

(1)

The
(resp. restricted) braid variety
associated to
\beta
is a closed subvariety of
\mathbb{A}^{\ell}
:

(1.4.4)

X(\beta):=f_{\ell}^{-1}(B);\quad\text{resp. }X(\beta,C):=\mathfrak{\mu mon}^{-1}(C),C\in T,\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathfrak{\mu mon}:=D\circ f_{\ell}:X(\beta)\rightarrow B\rightarrow T.

By Lemma
1.13
,
X(\beta)
(resp.
X(\beta,C)
) depends only on
\beta\in\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}
, up to a canonical isomorphism.

(2)

b\in B

acts
on

as follows:
\widehat{\vec{\epsilon}}=(\widehat{\epsilon}_{\ell},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\widehat{\epsilon}_{1}):=b\cdot\vec{\epsilon}
is uniquely determined by

(1.4.5)

[\mathrm{B}_{i_{\ell}}(\epsilon_{\ell})]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})]\circ[b^{-1}]=[\widetilde{b}_{\ell}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{\ell}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{\ell})]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{1})]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n},\widetilde{b}_{\ell}\in B.

That is, in
[b^{-1}|\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})|\text{\tiny$\cdots$}|\mathrm{B}_{i_{\ell}}(\epsilon_{\ell})]
, push
[b^{-1}]
by elementary moves (Figure
1.3.3
) to the right as far as possible, the outcome is
[\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{1})|\text{\tiny$\cdots$}|\mathrm{B}_{i_{\ell}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{\ell})|\widetilde{b}_{\ell}]
.

Definition 1.15

(

)
.

Let

. If for any

\displaystyle p_{m}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}\penalty\ (\text{go-up})&\text{if }\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}>p_{m-1},\\
\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}\penalty\ (\text{go-down})\text{ or }p_{m-1}\penalty\ (\text{stay})&\text{ if }\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}<p_{m-1},\end{array}\right.

and

, we say

walk
of
\beta
. Denote:

U_{p}:=\{\text{go-up's}\},\penalty\ \penalty\ S_{p}:=\{\text{stays}\},\penalty\ \penalty\ D_{p}:=\{\text{go-down's}\}.\Rightarrow[\ell]=\{1,\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\ell\}=U_{p}\sqcup D_{p}\sqcup S_{p}.

By a length count:
|U_{p}|-|D_{p}|=\ell(p_{\ell})-\ell(p_{0})=0
. Denote
\mathcal{W}(\beta):=\{\text{walks of }\beta\}
.

For any
1\leq m\leq\ell=\ell(\beta)
, denote

(1.4.9)

\mathrm{s}_{<m}(\beta):=\text{\tiny$\prod_{q=m-1}^{1}$}\mathrm{s}_{i_{q}},\quad\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta):=\text{\tiny$\prod_{q=\ell}^{m+1}$}\mathrm{s}_{i_{q}}.

We use
Convention

1.4
. Recall that
x^{y}=yxy^{-1}
in

Proposition 1.16
.

We have
B

(1.4.10)

\displaystyle X(\beta)=\sqcup_{p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta)}X_{p}(\beta),\quad\varphi:X_{p}(\beta):=X_{p}^{\ell}(\beta)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}\times\mathbb{K}^{|U_{p}|}:\vec{\epsilon}\mapsto(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})_{m\in S_{p}\sqcup U_{p}},

\displaystyle X(\beta,C)=\sqcup_{p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta)}X_{p}(\beta,C),\quad X_{p}(\beta,C):=X_{p}(\beta)\cap X(\beta,C),

such that

(1)

The inherited action of
b\in B
on

is identified with

In particular,
\det(\mathfrak{\mu mon}((\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})_{m\in S_{p}\sqcup U_{p}}))=(-1)^{|S_{p}|}
.

Proof.

By diagram calculus, the proof is straightforward. See Appendix

for the details.

2.
Cell decomposition of character varieties

In this section, we prove a strong form (Theorem
2.10
) of A. Mellit’s cell decomposition for very generic character varieties
[
40
]
.
This will be used to prove our main theorem
3.10
.

Recall that we have obtained a decomposition (see (
1.2.8
),(
1.2.7
)):

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{\vec{w}\in W^{2g}\times\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}W/W(C_{i})}$}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}),\quad\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})=M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})/PB_{\mathrm{par}},

and the defining equation of
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
has been interpreted via braid matrix diagrams as (
1.3.14
):

[\mathrm{M}_{\vec{w}}]=\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}([A_{2j-1}]\circ[A_{2j}]\circ[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[A_{2j}^{-1}])\circ\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}([x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}]^{\prime})\circ[u_{k}]\circ[C_{k}]\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{w}$}}{\sim}\mathsf{id}_{n}\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

To obtain the actual cell decomposition of
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
, we would like to decompose
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})
further. This amounts to decomposing
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
equivariantly.
For that, as mentioned in the end of Section
1.3
, the next step is to canonicalize
[\mathrm{M}_{\vec{w}}]
by diagram calculus.
This will be done in the next three subsections: Section
2.1
and Section
2.2
do the puncture and genus calculations respectively; Section
2.3
combines these local calculations to describe
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
via braid varieties.
Finally, in Section
2.4
, we prove a strong form (Theorem
2.10
) of the cell decomposition for
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
.

To simplify our computations, we do a
trick
as follows.

•

Fix an embedding
S_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}\subset\mathrm{FBD}_{n}
lifting
[-]:S_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}
(as a map of sets).

•

Via Lemma
1.11
, we also fix an embedding
[-]:B=U\rtimes T\hookrightarrow\mathrm{FBD}_{n}
(not as a morphism of monoids) lifting
[-]:B\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
.

Definition/Proposition 2.1
.

Fix
\mathcal{S}\subset[N]=\{1,\text{\tiny$\cdots$},N\}
. Take
Y_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},Y_{N}
such that:
Y_{i}\subset B
is a locally closed
\mathbb{K}
-subvariety for
i\notin\mathcal{S}
, and
Y_{i}
is a single permutation
2
2

2

By a little abuse of notations, we identify
w\in S_{n}
with
\{w\}\subset S_{n}
. Similarly, we identify
b\in B
with
\{b\}\subset B
.
in
S_{n}
, for
i\in\mathcal{S}
.

(1)

For any
\mathbb{K}
-variety
Z
, define

[Y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[Y_{N}]\times Z:=\{[y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[y_{N}]\in\mathrm{FBD}_{n}:y_{i}\in Y_{i}\}\times Z.

As a
\mathbb{K}
-variety,
[Y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[Y_{N}]\times Z
is
\text{\tiny$\prod_{i\notin\mathcal{S}}$}Y_{i}\times Z
. Define the obvious composition

E:[Y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[Y_{N}]\times Z\rightarrow[Y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[Y_{N}]\hookrightarrow\mathrm{FBD}_{n}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

(2)

If
\varphi:[Y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[Y_{N}]\times Z\rightarrow[Y_{1}^{\prime}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[Y_{M}^{\prime}]\times Z^{\prime}
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties respecting
E
, we say
\varphi
is
elementary
. In this case, we write

[Y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[Y_{N}]\times Z\cong[Y_{1}^{\prime}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[Y_{M}^{\prime}]\times Z^{\prime}.

In particular, any elementary isomorphism respects the maps

\displaystyle g_{-}:[Y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[Y_{N}]\times Z\rightarrow G=GL(n,\mathbb{K}):([y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[y_{N}],z)\mapsto y_{1}\text{\tiny$\cdots$}y_{N},

\displaystyle\beta_{-}:[Y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[Y_{N}]\times Z\rightarrow\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}:([y_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[y_{N}],z)\mapsto\beta_{[y_{1}]}\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ\beta_{[y_{N}]}.

(3)

Clearly, we have the following
elementary isomorphisms
:

(a)

Let
Y,Y_{1},Y_{2}\subset B
. If the multiplication induces an isomorphism
m:Y_{1}\times Y_{2}\xrightarrow{\cong}Y:(y_{1},y_{2})\mapsto y_{1}y_{2}
,
then
[Y_{1}]\circ[Y_{2}]\cong[Y]
.

(b)

If
Y_{1}\subset Y_{2}\subset B
and
Y_{2}
is a closed subgroup, then

[Y_{1}]\circ[Y_{2}]\cong[Y_{2}]\times Y_{1}\cong[Y_{2}]\circ[Y_{1}]
.

(c)

If
w\in S_{n}
and
Y\subset U_{w}^{+}
, then

[w]\circ[Y]\cong[wYw^{-1}]\circ[w]
.

(d)

For any
C\in T
and any
Y\subset B
, we have

[C]\circ[Y]\cong[CYC^{-1}]\circ[C]
.

2.1.
Diagram calculus for punctures

Back to the end of Section
1.3
. Recall (
1.3.10
), (
1.3.12
):

\displaystyle U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}\times N_{i}\times Z(C_{i})\cong B\dot{w}_{i}P_{i}:(\nu_{i},n_{i},z_{i})\mapsto x_{i}=\nu_{i}\dot{w}_{i}n_{i}z_{i};\penalty\ \penalty\ N_{i}\cong N_{i}:n_{i}\mapsto n_{i}^{\prime}=n_{i}C_{i}n_{i}^{-1}C_{i}.

\displaystyle[x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}]^{\prime}=[\nu_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[n_{i}^{\prime}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[\nu_{i}^{-1}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

Using the notations in (
1.3.7
), define
{}^{+}n_{i}^{\prime}:=R_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}(n_{i}^{\prime})\in U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i}
by (
1.3.9
).

Lemma 2.2
.

For any
D_{i+1}\in T
, we have a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties of the form

(2.1.1)

B\dot{w}_{i}P_{i}\times U\xrightarrow{\simeq}U\times U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}\times U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}\times(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})\times Z(C_{i}):(x_{i},u_{i+1})\mapsto(u_{i},\xi_{i}^{\prime},\xi_{i},{}^{+}n_{i}^{\prime},z_{i})

such that

(2.1.2)

[x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}]^{\prime}\circ[u_{i+1}]\circ[D_{i+1}]=[u_{i}]\circ[D_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[\xi_{i}^{\prime}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[\xi_{i}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n},\penalty\ \exists!\penalty\ D_{i}=C_{i}^{\dot{w}_{i}}D_{i+1}\in T.

Moreover, the above equation uniquely determines
(u_{i},\xi_{i}^{\prime},\xi_{i})\in U\times U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}\times U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}
.

Note
: Up to a canonical isomorphism, we have

[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[\xi_{i}^{\prime}]=[\mathrm{B}_{[\dot{w}_{i}]}(\xi_{i}^{\prime})]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n},\quad[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[\xi_{i}]=[\mathrm{B}_{[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]}(\xi_{i})]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

This will pave the way to braid varieties.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Observe that we have an isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties

B\dot{w}_{i}P_{i}\times U\xrightarrow{\simeq}U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}\times N_{i}\times Z(C_{i})\times U:(x_{i},u_{i+1})\mapsto(\nu_{i},n_{i},z_{i},u_{i+1}^{\prime}:=\nu_{i}^{-1}u_{i+1}).

such that
[x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}]^{\prime}\circ[u_{i+1}]=[\nu_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[n_{i}^{\prime}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[u_{i+1}^{\prime}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
.
By our trick (Definition/Proposition
2.1
), it suffices to compute

[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[N_{i}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[U]\circ[D_{i+1}]\ni[\nu_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[n_{i}^{\prime}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[u_{i+1}^{\prime}]\circ[D_{i+1}]
:

\displaystyle[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[N_{i}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[U]\circ[D_{i+1}]

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[U]\circ[D_{i+1}]\penalty\ (n_{i}^{\prime}={}^{-}n_{i}^{\prime}\cdot{}^{+}n_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}C_{i}^{-1}(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})C_{i}\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[U]\circ[D_{i+1}]

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[U]\circ[D_{i+1}]\times(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})\penalty\ (\text{direct factor}:{}^{+}n_{i}^{\prime}\in U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{+}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[D_{i+1}]\times(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})\penalty\ (U=U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{+}U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[D_{i+1}]\times(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[D_{i+1}]\times(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})\penalty\ (U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}=U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}]\circ[C_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[D_{i+1}]\times(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U]\circ[D_{i}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}]\times(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i}).

The uniqueness part is clear by definition of
\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

The upshot is that, Lemma
2.2
provides the inductive step for the diagram calculus for punctures. In our case, we have seen
u_{k}\in U
, take
D_{k}:=C_{k}
, and define
D_{i}
’s inductively as above.
Thus,

(2.1.3)

D_{1}=(\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}C_{i}^{\dot{w}_{i}})C_{k}\in T.

Altogether, the defining equation (
1.3.14
) for
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
reduces to:

(2.1.4)

[\mathrm{M}_{\vec{w}}]=\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}([A_{2j-1}]\circ[A_{2j}]\circ[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[A_{2j}])\circ[u_{1}]\circ[D_{1}]\circ\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}([\dot{w}_{i}\xi_{i}^{\prime}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}\xi_{i}])\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{w}$}}{\sim}\mathsf{id}_{n}.

For the diagram calculus for genera below, denote
u^{g}:=u_{1}\in U,\quad D^{g}:=D_{1}\in T
.

2.2.
Diagram calculus for genera

For each
1\leq j\leq g
, take the isomorphisms

(2.2.1)

\displaystyle U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\text{\tiny$\times$}T\text{\tiny$\times$}U\cong B\tau_{2j-1}B:(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},\eta_{2j-1})\mapsto A_{2j-1}=\mu_{2j-1}\tau_{2j-1}y_{2j-1}\eta_{2j-1},

\displaystyle U\text{\tiny$\times$}T\text{\tiny$\times$}U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}\cong B\tau_{2j}B:(\eta_{2j},y_{2j},\mu_{2j})\mapsto A_{2j}=\eta_{2j}y_{2j}\tau_{2j}\mu_{2j}.

Here, recall that
A_{m}\in B\tau_{m}B
,
\forall 1\leq m\leq 2g
. By Proposition
1.10
, we have

\displaystyle[A_{2j-1}]=[\mu_{2j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[\eta_{2j-1}],\penalty\ [A_{2j}]=[\eta_{2j}]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[\mu_{2j}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n},

\displaystyle[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]=[\eta_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\mu_{2j-1}^{-1}],\penalty\ [A_{2j}^{-1}]=[\mu_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[\eta_{2j}^{-1}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

Lemma 2.3
.

For any
D^{j}\in T
, we have a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties

(2.2.2)

\displaystyle B\tau_{2j-1}B\times B\tau_{2j}B\times U\xrightarrow{\simeq}U\times T^{2}\times(U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-})\times(U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+}),

\displaystyle(A_{2j-1},A_{2j},u^{j})\mapsto(u^{j-1},y_{2j-1},y_{2j},\zeta_{2j-1}^{\prime},\zeta_{2j}^{\prime},\zeta_{2j-1},\zeta_{2j},{}^{+}n^{2j},{}^{+}n^{2j-1}),

such that

(2.2.3)

\displaystyle[A_{2j-1}]\circ[A_{2j}]\circ[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[A_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[u^{j}]\circ[D^{j}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[u^{j-1}]\circ[D^{j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[\zeta_{2j-1}^{\prime}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[\zeta_{2j}^{\prime}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\zeta_{2j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[\zeta_{2j}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n},

for a unique
D^{j-1}\in T
:

(2.2.4)

D^{j-1}:=y_{2j-1}^{\tau_{2j-1}}y_{2j}^{\tau_{2j-1}}(y_{2j-1}^{-1})^{\tau_{2j-1}\tau_{2j}}(y_{2j}^{-1})^{\tau_{2j-1}\tau_{2j}\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}\tau_{2j}^{-1}}(D^{j})^{\tau_{2j-1}\tau_{2j}\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}\tau_{2j}^{-1}}\in T.

Moreover, (
2.2.3
) uniquely determines
(u^{j-1},\zeta_{2j-1}^{\prime},\zeta_{2j}^{\prime},\zeta_{2j-1},\zeta_{2j})\in U\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}
.

This provides the inductive step for the diagram calculus for genera.
In our case, we have seen that
u^{g}\in U
, take
D^{g}=D_{1}\in T
, and define
D^{j}
’s inductively as above. Thus,

(2.2.5)

D^{0}((y_{j})_{j=1}^{2g})=\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}(y_{2j-1}^{\tau_{2j-1}}(y_{2j-1}^{-1})^{\tau_{2j-1}\tau_{2j}}y_{2j}^{\tau_{2j-1}}(y_{2j}^{-1})^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2j-1},\tau_{2j}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}})^{\prod_{m=1}^{j-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2m-1},\tau_{2m}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}}D_{1}^{\prod_{m=1}^{g}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2m-1},\tau_{2m}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}}.

Altogether, the defining equation (
1.3.14
) for
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
, i.e.
[\mathrm{M}_{\vec{w}}]\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{w}$}}{\sim}\mathsf{id}_{n}\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
, reduces to:

[u^{0}D^{0}]\circ\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}([\tau_{2j-1}\zeta_{2j-1}^{\prime}]\circ[\tau_{2j}\zeta_{2j}^{\prime}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}\zeta_{2j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}\zeta_{2j}])\circ\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}([\dot{w}_{i}\xi_{i}^{\prime}]\circ[\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}\xi_{i}])\overset{\text{\tiny$\mathfrak{w}$}}{\sim}\mathsf{id}_{n}.

Proof of Lemma
2.3
.

As one could expect, the proof is done by diagram calculus.

Step
1
.
Denote
u^{\prime j}:=\eta_{2j}^{-1}u^{j}\in U,\eta_{2j}^{\prime}:=\eta_{2j-1}\eta_{2j}\in U,\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime}:=\eta_{2j-1}\mu_{2j}^{-1}\in U
.
So,
\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime-1}=\mu_{2j}\eta_{2j-1}^{-1}
.
Then
(A_{2j-1},A_{2j},u^{j})\mapsto(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime},\eta_{2j}^{\prime},y_{2j},\mu_{2j},u^{\prime j})
defines an isomorphism

B\tau_{2j-1}B\times B\tau_{2j}B\times U\xrightarrow{\cong}U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times T\times U^{2}\times T\times U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}\times U

such that we obtain an identity in
\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
:

(2.2.6)

\displaystyle[A_{2j-1}]\circ[A_{2j}]\circ[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[A_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[u^{j}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\mu_{2j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[\eta_{2j}^{\prime}]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]

\displaystyle\circ[\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\mu_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\mu_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[u^{\prime j}].

Note
: The variable
\mu_{2j}^{-1}\in U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}
becomes free (appears only once).

Step
2
.
We firstly compute
[\mu_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\mu_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[u^{\prime j}]
.
By Definition/Proposition
2.1
,

\displaystyle[\mu_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[U]\cong[\mu_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[U]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[\mu_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]\quad(U=U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[\mu_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}}^{+}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]\cong[\mu_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}].

This means we obtain an isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties

(2.2.7)

\displaystyle U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times T\times U^{2}\times T\times U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}\times U\xrightarrow{\cong}U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times T\times U^{3}\times T\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-},

\displaystyle(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime},\eta_{2j}^{\prime},y_{2j},\mu_{2j},u^{\prime j})\mapsto(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime},\eta_{2j}^{\prime},u_{3}^{j},y_{2j},L_{1}^{-}),

such that

[\mu_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\mu_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[u^{\prime j}]=[u_{3}^{j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[L_{1}^{-}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

Hence,

(2.2.8)

\displaystyle[A_{2j-1}]\circ[A_{2j}]\circ[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[A_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[u^{j}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\mu_{2j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[\eta_{2j}^{\prime}]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]

\displaystyle\circ[\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[u_{3}^{j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[L_{1}^{-}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}].

Note
: the non-torus variables
\mu_{2j-1}\in U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}
,
\eta_{2j}^{\prime},\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime-1},u_{3}^{j}\in U
,
L_{1}^{-}\in U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}
all become free.

Step
3
.
By our trick (Definition/Proposition
2.1
), the computation of
[A_{2j-1}]\circ[A_{2j}]\circ[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[A_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[u^{j}]
then reduces to that of the following variety in
\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
:

(2.2.9)

[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[U]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[U]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}].

The idea is ‘canonicalize’. According to the expression above, reorder the variables:

(2.2.10)

\displaystyle U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times T\times U^{3}\times T\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}\xrightarrow{\cong}U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times(T\times U)^{2}\times U\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-},

\displaystyle(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime},\eta_{2j}^{\prime},u_{3}^{j},y_{2j},L_{1}^{-})\mapsto(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},\eta_{2j}^{\prime},y_{2j},\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime-1},u_{3}^{j},L_{1}^{-})

Step
3.1
. We firstly compute
[U]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U]\ni[\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[u_{3}^{j}]
:

\displaystyle[U]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U]\cong[U]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{+}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}]\penalty\ (U=U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{+}U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+}]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}]\cong[U]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}]\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+},

with the direct factor
U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+}\ni{}^{+}n^{2j-1}
.
This means we obtain an isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties

(2.2.11)

\displaystyle U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times(T\times U)^{2}\times U\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}\xrightarrow{\simeq}U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times(T\times U)^{2}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+},

\displaystyle(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},\eta_{2j}^{\prime},y_{2j},\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime-1},u_{3}^{j},L_{1}^{-})\mapsto(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},\eta_{2j}^{\prime},y_{2j},u_{4}^{j},L_{3}^{-},L_{1}^{-},{}^{+}n^{2j-1}),

such that

[\eta_{2j-1}^{\prime-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[u_{3}^{j}]=[u_{4}^{j}]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[L_{3}^{-}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

Step
3.2
. We compute
[U]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[U]\ni[\eta_{2j}^{\prime}]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[u_{4}^{j}]
, which is similar:

\displaystyle[U]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[U]\cong[U]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}}^{+}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}]\penalty\ \penalty\ (U=U_{\tau_{2j}}^{+}U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}]\cong[U]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}]\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+},

with the direct factor
U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}\ni{}^{+}n^{2j}
.
This means we obtain an isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties

\displaystyle U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times(T\times U)^{2}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+}\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times T\times U\times T\times U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+},

\displaystyle(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},\eta_{2j}^{\prime},y_{2j},u_{4}^{j},L_{3}^{-},L_{1}^{-},{}^{+}n^{2j-1})\mapsto(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},u_{5}^{j},y_{2j},L_{5}^{-},L_{3}^{-},L_{1}^{-},{}^{+}n^{2j},{}^{+}n^{2j-1}),

such that

[\eta_{2j}^{\prime}]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[u_{4}^{j}]=[u_{5}^{j}]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[L_{5}^{-}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

Step
3.3
. Now, we compute
[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[U]\ni[\mu_{2j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[u_{5}^{j}]
:

\displaystyle[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[U]\cong[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{-}]

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{+}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{-}]\cong[U]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{-}].

This means we obtain an isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties

\displaystyle U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times T\times U\times T\times U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+}\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle U\times T\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{-}\times T\times U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+},

\displaystyle(\mu_{2j-1},y_{2j-1},u_{5}^{j},y_{2j},L_{5}^{-},L_{3}^{-},L_{1}^{-},{}^{+}n^{2j},{}^{+}n^{2j-1})\mapsto(u^{j-1},y_{2j-1},L_{7}^{-},y_{2j},L_{5}^{-},L_{3}^{-},L_{1}^{-},{}^{+}n^{2j},{}^{+}n^{2j-1}),

such that

[\mu_{2j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[u_{5}^{j}]=[u^{j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[L_{7}^{-}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

In summary, we have obtained

(2.2.12)

\displaystyle[A_{2j-1}]\circ[A_{2j}]\circ[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[A_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[u^{j}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[u^{j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}L_{7}^{-}y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[L_{5}^{-}y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[L_{3}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[L_{1}^{-}y_{2j}^{-1}].

and

[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}]\circ[U]\circ[y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[U]\circ[y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[y_{2j}^{-1}]\cong[U]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{-}y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-}y_{2j}^{-1}]\times U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+}
.

Step
4
. Deal with the torus variables.
Now, for any
D^{j}\in T
, it follows that

\displaystyle[A_{2j-1}]\circ[A_{2j}]\circ[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[A_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[u^{j}]\circ[D^{j}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[u^{j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[y_{2j-1}L_{7}^{-}y_{2j}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[L_{5}^{-}y_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[L_{3}^{-}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[L_{1}^{-}y_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[D^{j}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[u^{j-1}]\circ[D^{j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}]\circ[\zeta_{2j-1}^{\prime}]\circ[\tau_{2j}]\circ[\zeta_{2j}^{\prime}]\circ[\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}]\circ[\zeta_{2j-1}]\circ[\tau_{2j}^{-1}]\circ[\zeta_{2j}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n},

where

, and

is indeed given by (

).

In other words, we have obtained an isomorphism of the form (

) in Lemma

such that (

) and (

) hold.
By definition of
\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}
, the uniqueness part is clear.
Done.

Lemma 2.5
.

The induced action of

on

1.14

\widehat{y}_{2j-1}=D(b)^{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}D(b)^{-1}y_{2j-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ \widehat{y}_{2j}=D(b)(D(b)^{\tau_{2j}})^{-1}y_{2j}.

b)

If

\displaystyle{}^{+}\widehat{n}_{i}^{\prime}=t^{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}{}^{+}n_{i}^{\prime}(t^{-1})^{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}};{}^{+}\widehat{n}^{2j-1}=t{}^{+}n^{2j-1}t^{-1};{}^{+}\widehat{n}^{2j}=t{}^{+}n^{2j}t^{-1},

\displaystyle(\widehat{y}_{2j-1}=t^{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}t^{-1}y_{2j-1};\widehat{y}_{2j}=t(t^{\tau_{2j}})^{-1}y_{2j};\widehat{u}^{0}=tu^{0}t^{-1};\widehat{D}^{0}=tD^{0}(t^{-1})^{\prod_{m=1}^{g}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2m-1},\tau_{2m}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}})

\displaystyle[\widehat{u}^{0}]\circ[\widehat{D}^{0}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{\beta(\vec{w})}(\widehat{\vec{\epsilon}})]^{\prime}=[t]\circ[u^{0}]\circ[D^{0}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{\beta(\vec{w})}(\vec{\epsilon})]^{\prime}\circ[t^{-1}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

(\widehat{u}^{0},\widehat{D}^{0},\widehat{\vec{\epsilon}})
.

Proof.

Now,
\widehat{A}_{j}=bA_{j}b^{-1}
,
\widehat{x}_{i}=bx_{i}
.
So,
[\widehat{A}_{j}]=[b]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[A_{j}]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[b^{-1}]
,
[\widehat{x}_{i}C_{i}\widehat{x}_{i}^{-1}]^{\prime}=[b]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}]^{\prime}\text{\tiny$\circ$}[b^{-1}]
.

a)
.
By Section
2.2
,

Thus,

, as desired.
Similarly,
\widehat{y}_{2j}=D(b)y_{2j}(D(b)^{-1})^{\tau_{2j}}\in T
.

Recall that
\widehat{u}_{k}=bu_{k}(b^{C_{k}})^{-1}
,
D_{k}=C_{k}
, then we have equalities in

:

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle[\widehat{u}^{0}\widehat{D}^{0}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{\beta(\vec{w})}(\widehat{\vec{\epsilon}})]=[\widehat{\mathrm{M}}_{\vec{w}}]=\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}([\widehat{A}_{2j-1}]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[\widehat{A}_{2j}]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[\widehat{A}_{2j-1}^{-1}]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[\widehat{A}_{2j}^{-1}])\text{\tiny$\circ$}\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}[\widehat{x}_{i}C_{i}\widehat{x}_{i}^{-1}]^{\prime}\text{\tiny$\circ$}[\widehat{u}_{k}D_{k}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[b]\text{\tiny$\circ$}\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}([A_{2j-1}]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[A_{2j}]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[A_{2j-1}^{-1}]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[A_{2j}^{-1}])\text{\tiny$\circ$}\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}[x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}]^{\prime}\text{\tiny$\circ$}[b^{-1}]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[\widehat{u}_{k}D_{k}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[b]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[u^{0}D^{0}]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[\mathrm{B}_{\beta(\vec{w})}(\vec{\epsilon})]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[D_{k}^{-1}u_{k}^{-1}b^{-1}\widehat{u}_{k}D_{k}]=[b]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[u^{0}D^{0}]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[\mathrm{B}_{\beta(\vec{w})}(\vec{\epsilon})]\text{\tiny$\circ$}[b^{-1}].

This equation uniquely determines
(\widehat{u}^{0},\widehat{D}^{0},\widehat{\vec{\epsilon}})
, and we see that
\widehat{\vec{\epsilon}}
coincides with the action of
b\in B
on
\vec{\epsilon}\in X(\beta(\vec{w}))
in Definition
1.14
. This show
a)
and most part of
b)
.

b)
. By above, it remains to check the action of
b=t\in T
on
(({}^{+}n^{j})_{j},({}^{+}n_{i}^{\prime})_{i})
.

By the equation
\widehat{\nu}_{i}\dot{w}_{i}\widehat{n}_{i}\widehat{z}_{i}=\widehat{x}_{i}=tx_{i}=t\nu_{i}\dot{w}_{i}n_{i}z_{i}
, we see that
\widehat{n}_{i}=t^{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}n_{i}(t^{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}})^{-1}
. It follows that

\widehat{n}_{i}^{\prime}=\widehat{n}_{i}C_{i}\widehat{n}_{i}^{-1}C_{i}^{-1}=t^{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}n_{i}^{\prime}(t^{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}})^{-1}
. Then

{}^{+}\widehat{n}_{i}^{\prime}=R_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}(\widehat{n}_{i}^{\prime})=t^{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}{}^{+}n_{i}^{\prime}(t^{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}})^{-1},

as desired.

It remains to compute

.
By (

) and similar to (

.

) and induction, we get

.
By the equation
\widehat{\mu}_{2j-1}\tau_{2j-1}\widehat{y}_{2j-1}\widehat{\eta}_{2j-1}=t\mu_{2j-1}\tau_{2j-1}y_{2j-1}\eta_{2j-1}t^{-1}
, we see

,
\widehat{\eta}_{2j-1}=t\eta_{2j-1}t^{-1}
.
Similarly,

,

. Now, by Remark

, we see

, as desired.

2.4.
The cell decomposition

Recall that
\pi_{\vec{w}}^{\prime\prime}:M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})=M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})/PB
is a principal
PB
-bundle. Moreover,

M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})\cong\widetilde{X}(\beta(\vec{w}))\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}(U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+})\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})

by (
2.3.7
). Say,

\beta:=\beta(\vec{w})=\sigma_{i_{\ell}}\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\sigma_{i_{1}}\in\mathrm{FBr}_{n}^{+}
.
Define a
B
-invariant closed subvariety of
T^{2g}\times X(\beta)
by

(2.4.1)

\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta):=\widetilde{X}(\beta)\cap(T^{2g}\times X_{p}(\beta))\subset T^{2g}\times X(\beta),\quad p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta).

Then by Lemma
2.5
, the
B
-equivariant decomposition
\widetilde{X}(\beta)=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta)}$}\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)
induces a (resp.
B
-equivariant) decomposition of

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})
(resp.
M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})
), as desired. It remains to give a more concrete description of each piece in these decompositions. This reduces to describe
\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)
.

We start with the following observation:
Remember that
PB
acts freely on
M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})
.
By (
2.3.10
), we then see that
PT=T/\mathbb{K}^{\times}\subset PB
preserves and acts
freely
on

\widetilde{X}(\beta)\cong\widetilde{X}(\beta)\times\{0\}\subset M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})
.
It turns out that, this free action leads to a more concrete description of
\widetilde{X}(\beta)
, which we now pursue.

By Proposition
1.16
,

X_{p}(\beta)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}\times\mathbb{K}^{|U_{p}|}:(\vec{\epsilon})=(\epsilon_{m})_{m=\ell}^{1}\mapsto(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})_{m\in S_{p}\sqcup U_{p}}
.
Define

(2.4.2)

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p}:T^{2g}\times(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}\rightarrow T_{1}:(\vec{y}=(y_{j})_{j=1}^{2g},(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})_{m\in S_{p}})\mapsto D^{0}(\vec{y})\mathfrak{\mu mon}((\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})_{m\in S_{p}}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta):=\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p}^{-1}(I_{n})\subset T^{2g}\times(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}.

Here,
D^{0}(\vec{y})
is given by (
2.2.5
), and
\mathfrak{\mu mon}((\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})_{m\in S_{p}})
is given by Proposition
1.16
(2).
Clearly,
\phi_{\vec{w},p}:=\phi_{\vec{w}}|_{T^{2g}\times X_{p}(\beta)}
is the composition of
\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p}
with the obvious projection:

\phi_{\vec{w},p}:T^{2g}\times X_{p}(\beta)\cong T^{2g}\times(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}\times\mathbb{K}^{|U_{p}|}\twoheadrightarrow T^{2g}\times(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}\xrightarrow{\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p}}T_{1}.

Hence, we have

(2.4.3)

\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)=\phi_{\vec{w},p}^{-1}(I_{n})=\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)\times\mathbb{K}^{|U_{p}|}.

In particular,
\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)\neq\varnothing
if and only
\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)\neq\varnothing
.

Also, by Proposition
1.16
and (
2.3.9
),
T^{2g}\times(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}
inherits an action of
T
such that, the projection
T^{2g}\times X_{p}(\beta)\cong T^{2g}\times(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}\times\mathbb{K}^{|U_{p}|}\twoheadrightarrow T^{2g}\times(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}
is equivariant with respect to the projection
B\rightarrow T:b\mapsto D(b)
.
Besides, observe that

(2.4.4)

\text{$PT$ acts freely on $\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)$ if and only if it does so on $\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)$}.

Indeed, this is the case if
\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)\neq\varnothing
by the observation above.

The key property is the following

Lemma 2.6
.

The
PT
-action on
\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)
is free if and only if
\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p}:T^{2g}\times(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}\rightarrow T_{1}
is surjective.

Proof.

Firstly, we consider the
PT
-action. For that, we make some preparations:

(1)

For any
\tau\in S_{n}
, define a subgroup of
T
by

(2.4.5)

{}^{\tau}T:=\{t\in T:t^{\tau}=t,\text{ i.e., }t_{a}=t_{\tau(a)},\forall a\in[n]\}\subset T.

So,
{}^{(a\penalty\ b)}T=\{t\in T:t_{a}=t_{b}\}
for any transposition
(a\penalty\ b)\in S_{n}
.
Define a subgroup of
T
by

(2.4.6)

{}^{I}T:=\{t\in T:t^{\tau}=t,\forall\tau\in I\}\subset T,\penalty\ \penalty\ I\subset S_{n};\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ {}^{I}T=\cap_{\tau\in I}{}^{\tau}T=\cap_{a\in[n],\tau\in I}{}^{(a\penalty\ \tau(a))}T.

(2)

By definition,
{}^{\tau}T={}^{\tau^{-1}}T
. Also,
t=t^{\tau_{1}},t=t^{\tau_{2}}
implies that
t=t^{\tau_{1}}=(t^{\tau_{2}})^{\tau_{1}}=t^{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}
, so by (1),

(2.4.7)

{}^{I}T={}^{\langle I\rangle}T={}^{\overline{\langle I\rangle}}T.

Here, we denote by
\langle I\rangle\subset S_{n}
the subgroup generated by
I
, and

(2.4.8)

\overline{\langle I\rangle}:=\langle(a\penalty\ \tau(a)),\forall a\in[n],\tau\in I\rangle\subset S_{n}.

(3)

Let
[n]=O_{1}\sqcup\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\sqcup O_{r}
be any partition, denoted by
\vec{O}
. Define subgroups of
T
and
S_{n}
:

(2.4.9)

{}^{\vec{O}}T:=\{t\in T:a,b\in O_{m}\Rightarrow t_{a}=t_{b},\forall 1\leq m\leq r\}\subset T;\quad S_{\vec{O}}:=S_{|O_{1}|}\times\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\times S_{|O_{r}|}\subset S_{n}.

Then for any subset
I\subset S_{n}
, we have

(2.4.10)

{}^{I}T={}^{\vec{O}}T\Leftrightarrow\text{ $\{O_{i}:1\leq i\leq r\}=\{\langle I\rangle$-orbits on $[n]\}$ }\Leftrightarrow\overline{\langle I\rangle}=S_{\vec{O}}.

Now, we see that
{}^{I}T={}^{J}I
if and only if
\overline{\langle I\rangle}=\overline{\langle J\rangle}
.
In particular,
{}^{I}T=\mathbb{K}^{\times}I_{n}
if and only if
\overline{\langle I\rangle}=S_{n}
.

Come back to the proof.
The free
PT
-action on
\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)
means:
\forall t\in T
,
\forall((y_{j})_{j=1}^{2g},(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})_{m\in S_{p}})\in\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)
, we have

t\cdot((y_{j})_{j=1}^{2g},(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})_{m\in S_{p}})=((y_{j})_{j=1}^{2g},(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})_{m\in S_{p}})\Rightarrow t\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}I_{n}
.
We use
Convention

1.4
.
Recall by (
2.3.10
) that,
\widehat{y}_{j}=y_{j}
if and only if
t^{\tau_{j}}=t
.
By Proposition
1.16
(1), for any

,
\widehat{\epsilon}_{m}^{\prime}=\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}
if and only if
(t^{p_{m-1}})_{i_{m}}=(t^{p_{m-1}})_{i_{m}+1}
.
Equivalently,
(t^{p_{m-1}})^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}=t^{p_{m-1}}
, i.e..

, where:

(2.4.11)

\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m}}:=p_{m-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}\in S_{n}.

Thus, denote

(2.4.12)

J_{p}:=\{\tau_{j}:1\leq j\leq 2g,\penalty\ \underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m}}:m\in S_{p}\}\subset S_{n}.

By the preparation above, we conclude that

(2.4.13)

\text{$PT$ acts freely on $\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)$ }\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ {}^{J_{p}}T=\mathbb{K}^{\times}I_{n}\Leftrightarrow\overline{\langle J_{p}\rangle}=S_{n}.

On the other hand, we consider the image of
\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p}
.
Again, we begin with some preparations:

(1)

For any
\tau\in S_{n}
, define a subgroup of
T_{1}
by

(2.4.14)

{}_{\tau}T:=\{y(y^{-1})^{\tau}:y\in T\}\subset T_{1}.

So,
{}_{(a\penalty\ b)}T=\{\mathrm{K}_{a}(\lambda)\mathrm{K}_{b}(\lambda^{-1}):\lambda\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}\}
for any transposition
(a\penalty\ b)\in S_{n}
.
For any

, define
{}_{I}T\subset T_{1}
as the subgroup generated by
{}_{\tau}T,\tau\in I
, and write

{}_{I}T:=\langle{}_{\tau}T:\tau\in I\rangle
.
Equivalently,

{}_{I}T=\langle{}_{(a\penalty\ \tau(a))}T:a\in[n],\tau\in I\rangle=\langle\mathrm{K}_{a}(\lambda)\mathrm{K}_{\tau(a)}(\lambda^{-1}):\lambda\in\mathbb{K}^{\times},a\in[n],\tau\in I\rangle\subset T_{1}.

(2)

By definition,
{}_{\tau}T={}_{\tau^{-1}}T
. Also, as
