y(y^{-1})^{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}=(y(y^{-1})^{\tau_{2}})(y^{\tau_{2}}((y^{-1})^{\tau_{2}})^{\tau_{1}})
, we have by (1):

{}_{I}T={}_{\langle I\rangle}T={}_{\overline{\langle I\rangle}}T.

(3)

Let
[n]=O_{1}\sqcup\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\sqcup O_{r}
be any partition, denoted by
\vec{O}
. Define a subgroup

{}_{\vec{O}}T:=\{t\in T:\text{\tiny$\prod_{q\in O_{a}}$}t_{q}=1,\forall 1\leq a\leq r\}\subset T_{1}.

Then for any subset
I\subset S_{n}
, we have

{}_{I}T={}_{\vec{O}}T\Leftrightarrow\text{ $\{O_{i}:1\leq i\leq r\}=\{\langle I\rangle$-orbits on $[n]\}$ }\Leftrightarrow\overline{\langle I\rangle}=S_{\vec{O}}.

Now, we see that
{}_{I}T={}_{J}I
if and only if
\overline{\langle I\rangle}=\overline{\langle J\rangle}
.
In particular,
{}_{I}T=T_{1}
if and only if
\overline{\langle I\rangle}=S_{n}
.

Now, come back to our setting.
Observe that
\mathrm{s}(\beta)=\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2j-1},\tau_{2j}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}
.
Inspired by Proposition
1.16
(2) and equation (
2.2.5
), we define an isomorphism

m_{p}:T_{1}\xrightarrow{\simeq}T_{1}:t\mapsto t(D_{1}^{-1})^{\mathrm{s}(\beta)}\text{\tiny$\prod_{m\in S_{p}}$}(\mathrm{K}_{i_{m}}(-1))^{\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)}.

So
\im(\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p})=T_{1}
if and only if
\im(m_{p}\circ\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p})=T_{1}
. It suffices to consider the latter.

For any
1\leq j\leq g
, denote

c_{<j}=c_{<j}(\vec{\tau}):=\text{\tiny$\prod_{m=1}^{j-1}$}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2m-1},\tau_{2m}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}.

In (
2.2.5
), observe that

\displaystyle(y_{2j-1}^{\tau_{2j-1}}(y_{2j-1}^{-1})^{\tau_{2j-1}\tau_{2j}})^{\prod_{m=1}^{j-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2m-1},\tau_{2m}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}}=\widetilde{y}_{2j-1}(\widetilde{y}_{2j-1}^{-1})^{c_{<j+1}\tau_{2j}c_{<j}^{-1}},\quad\widetilde{y}_{2j-1}:=y_{2j-1}^{c_{<j}\tau_{2j-1}}\in T,

\displaystyle(y_{2j}^{\tau_{2j-1}}(y_{2j}^{-1})^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2j-1},\tau_{2j}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}})^{\prod_{m=1}^{j-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2m-1},\tau_{2m}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}}=\widetilde{y}_{2j}(\widetilde{y}_{2j}^{-1})^{c_{<j+1}\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}c_{<j}^{-1}},\quad\widetilde{y}_{2j}=y_{2j}^{c_{<j}\tau_{2j-1}}\in T.

In Proposition
1.16
(2), for each
m\in S_{p}
, observe that

\displaystyle\{(\mathrm{K}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime-1})\mathrm{K}_{i_{m}+1}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}))^{\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)}=\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)(i_{m})}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime-1})\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)(i_{m}+1)}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}):\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{}_{(\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)(i_{m})\penalty\ \mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)(i_{m}+1))}T={}_{\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)^{-1}}T\subset T_{1}.

Now, by above, (
2.2.5
), and Proposition
1.16
(2), we have

\im(m_{p}\circ\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p})={}_{\widetilde{J}_{p}}I
, where

(2.4.15)

\widetilde{J}_{p}:=\{c_{<j+1}\tau_{2j}c_{<j}^{-1},c_{<j+1}\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}c_{<j}^{-1},1\leq j\leq g;\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)^{-1},m\in S_{p}\}.

Then by the preparation above, we conclude that

(2.4.16)

\im(m_{p}\circ\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p})={}_{\widetilde{J}_{p}}I=T_{1}\Leftrightarrow\overline{\langle\widetilde{J}_{p}\rangle}=S_{n}.

Combined with (
2.4.13
), this shows that the lemma is equivalent to the following statement:

\overline{\langle J_{p}\rangle}=S_{n}\Leftrightarrow\overline{\langle\widetilde{J}_{p}\rangle}=S_{n},

which will follow from the claim below.

Claim
: we have
\langle J_{p}\rangle=\langle\widetilde{J}_{p}\rangle
.

Proof of Claim
.
Denote

J_{\tau}:=\{\tau_{m}:1\leq m\leq 2g\}\subset S_{n},\quad\widetilde{J}_{\tau}:=\{\widetilde{\tau}_{2j}:=c_{<j+1}\tau_{2j}c_{<j}^{-1},\widetilde{\tau}_{2j-1}:=c_{<j+1}\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}c_{<j}^{-1},1\leq j\leq g\}\subset S_{n}.

Firstly, we prove by induction that, for each
1\leq j\leq g
, we have

(2.4.17)

\langle\tau_{m}:1\leq m\leq 2j\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{m}:1\leq m\leq 2j\rangle\subset S_{n}.

In particular,
j=g
gives
\langle J_{\tau}\rangle=\langle\widetilde{J}_{\tau}\rangle
.

For
j=1
, we have
\widetilde{\tau}_{2}=\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\tau_{1}^{-1},\quad\widetilde{\tau}_{1}=\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\tau_{1}^{-1}\tau_{2}^{-1}\tau_{1}^{-1}
.
So,
\widetilde{\tau}_{2}^{-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{1}\widetilde{\tau}_{2}=\tau_{1}^{-1}\in\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{1},\widetilde{\tau}_{2}\rangle
.
It follows that
\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{1},\widetilde{\tau}_{2}\rangle=\langle\tau_{1}^{-1},\widetilde{\tau}_{2}=\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\tau_{1}^{-1}\rangle=\langle\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\rangle
, as desired.

Suppose (
2.4.17
) holds for ‘
<j
’, so

c_{<j}\in\langle\tau_{m}:1\leq m\leq 2(j-1)\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{m}:1\leq m\leq 2(j-1)\rangle.

Observe that
\widetilde{\tau}_{2j}^{-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{2j-1}\widetilde{\tau}_{2j}=c_{<j}\tau_{2j}^{-1}\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2j-1},\tau_{2j}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}\tau_{2j}c_{<j}^{-1}=c_{<j}\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}c_{<j}^{-1}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{m}:1\leq m\leq 2j\rangle=\langle\tau_{m}:1\leq m\leq 2(j-1),c_{<j}\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}c_{<j}^{-1},\widetilde{\tau}_{2j}=c_{<j+1}\tau_{2j}c_{<j}^{-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\tau_{m}:1\leq m\leq 2(j-1),\tau_{2j-1}^{-1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2j-1},\tau_{2j}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}\tau_{2j}=\tau_{2j-1}\tau_{2j}\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}\rangle=\langle\tau_{m}:1\leq m\leq 2j\rangle.

This finishes the induction, and hence proves (
2.4.17
).

Now, observe that
\mathrm{s}(\beta)=\prod_{j=1}^{g}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{(}}\tau_{2j-1},\tau_{2j}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{)}}\in\langle\widetilde{J}_{\tau}\rangle=\langle J_{\tau}\rangle
.
Thus,

\langle\widetilde{J}_{p}\rangle=\langle J_{\tau},\mathrm{s}(\beta)^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)^{-1}\mathrm{s}(\beta),m\in S_{p}\rangle=\langle J_{\tau},\mathrm{s}_{<m}(\beta)^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{s}_{<m}(\beta)=:\widetilde{s}_{i_{m}},m\in S_{p}\rangle.

Say,
S_{p}=\{m_{1}<\text{\tiny$\cdots$}<m_{N}\}
.
Set
J_{s}:=\{\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m}}:m\in S_{p}\}\subset W=S_{n}
,
\widetilde{J}_{s}:=\{\widetilde{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m}}:m\in S_{p}\}\subset W=S_{n}
.
We will show that

(2.4.18)

\langle\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{j}}},1\leq j\leq L\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{j}}},1\leq j\leq L\rangle.

In particular,
L=N
gives
\langle J_{s}\rangle=\langle\widetilde{J}_{s}\rangle
, and the Claim will follow immediately.

For that, we firstly prove by induction that, for each
1\leq j\leq N
, we have

(2.4.19)

\text{\tiny$\prod_{q=j}^{1}$}\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{q}}}=p_{m_{j}-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{<m_{j}+1}(\beta).

For
j=1
, as
p_{m_{1}-1}=\mathrm{s}_{<m_{1}}(\beta)
, we get

\prod_{q=1}^{1}\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{q}}}=p_{m_{1}-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m_{1}}}\mathrm{s}_{<m_{1}}(\beta)=p_{m_{1}-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{<m_{1}+1}(\beta)
. Done.

Suppose (
2.4.19
) holds for ‘
\leq j
’, then

\text{\tiny$\prod_{q=j+1}^{1}$}\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{q}}}=\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{j+1}}}\text{\tiny$\prod_{q=j}^{1}$}\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{q}}}=(p_{m_{j+1}-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m_{j+1}}}p_{m_{j+1}-1})p_{m_{j}-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{<m_{j}+1}(\beta)=p_{m_{j+1}-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{<m_{j+1}+1}(\beta).

This finishes the induction, and hence proves (
2.4.19
).

Now, for any
1\leq j\leq N
, we have

\displaystyle(\text{\tiny$\prod_{q=j-1}^{1}$}\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{q}}})^{-1}\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{j}}}(\text{\tiny$\prod_{q=j-1}^{1}$}\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{q}}})=(p_{m_{j-1}-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{<m_{j-1}+1}(\beta))^{-1}p_{m_{j}-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m_{j}}}p_{m_{j}-1}(p_{m_{j-1}-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{<m_{j-1}+1}(\beta))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{<m_{j}}(\beta)^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m_{j}}}\mathrm{s}_{<m_{j}}(\beta)=\widetilde{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{j}}}.

It follows that for any
1\leq L\leq N
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{j}}},1\leq j\leq L\rangle=\langle(\text{\tiny$\prod_{q=j-1}^{1}$}\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{q}}})^{-1}\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{j}}}(\text{\tiny$\prod_{q=j-1}^{1}$}\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{q}}}),1\leq j\leq L\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m_{j}}},1\leq j\leq L\rangle.

This is exactly what we want, hence the claim follows. Done.
∎

Inspired by the lemma, we make the following

Definition 2.7
.

For
\beta=\beta(\vec{w})
, define

(2.4.20)

\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta):=\{p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta):\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)\neq\varnothing\},

and any
p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta)
is called an
admissible walk
.
By the proof of Lemma
2.6
,

p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta)\Leftrightarrow\langle J_{p}\rangle\text{ acts transitively on $[n]$ }\Leftrightarrow\overline{\langle J_{p}\rangle}=S_{n};\quad\forall p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta),

where
J_{p}=\{\tau_{j}:1\leq j\leq 2g,\penalty\ \underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m}}=p_{m-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}:m\in S_{p}\}\subset S_{n}
. Alternatively, denote

J_{p}^{\prime}:=\{\tau_{j}:1\leq j\leq 2g,\penalty\ \widetilde{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m}}=\mathrm{s}_{<m}(\beta)^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{s}_{<m}(\beta):m\in S_{p}\}\subset S_{n}.

We have seen that
\langle J_{p}\rangle=\langle J_{p}^{\prime}\rangle
, then the same holds if we replace
J_{p}
above by
J_{p}^{\prime}
.

As an immediate corollary of Lemma
2.6
, we obtain

Corollary 2.8
.

If
\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)\neq\varnothing
, then we have a (
B
-equivariant) isomorphism

(2.4.21)

\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)=\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)\times\mathbb{K}^{|U_{p}|}\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|+2gn-n+1}\times\mathbb{K}^{|U_{p}|}.

Proof.

If
\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)\neq\varnothing
, then we know that the
PT
-action on
\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)
is free.
By Lemma
2.6
, the map

\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p}:T^{2g}\times(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|}\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|+2gn}\rightarrow T_{1}\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{n-1}.

is surjective.
By (
2.3.3
), Proposition
1.16
(2), and (
2.2.5
), the composition
m_{p}\circ\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p}
is a surjective group homomorphism of algebraic tori. It follows that

\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)=\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p}^{-1}(I_{n})=(m_{p}\circ\overline{\phi}_{\vec{w},p})^{-1}((D_{1}^{-1})^{\mathrm{s}(\beta)}\text{\tiny$\prod_{m\in S_{p}}$}(\mathrm{K}_{i_{m}}(-1))^{\mathrm{s}_{>m}(\beta)})\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|+2gn-n+1}.

Thus,
\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)=\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta)\times\mathbb{K}^{|U_{p}|}\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|+2gn-n+1}\times\mathbb{K}^{|U_{p}|}
, as desired.
∎

Now, denote

(2.4.22)

n_{\vec{w}}:=\text{\tiny$\sum_{j=1}^{2g}$}|U_{\tau_{j}}^{+}|+\text{\tiny$\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}$}|U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i}|=2g|U|+\text{\tiny$\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}$}|N_{i}|-\frac{1}{2}\ell(\beta).

By Lemma
2.5
and Corollary
2.8
, we have shown the following

Proposition 2.9
.

We have a
B
-equivariant decomposition into locally closed
\mathbb{K}
-subvarieties

M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})=\sqcup_{p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta)}M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p),\quad M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p):=\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta)\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}(U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}\times U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+})\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i}).

In particular,

M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{a(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}=(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{|S_{p}|+2gn-n+1}\times\mathbb{K}^{|U_{p}|+n_{\vec{w}}},

and
a(\vec{w},p)+2b(\vec{w},p)=4g|U|+2\text{\tiny$\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}$}|N_{i}|+2gn-n+1

is a constant independent of
\vec{w},p
.

Recall by (
2.3.8
) that,
PB
acts freely on
M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})
and

\pi_{\vec{w}}^{\prime\prime}:M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})=M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})/PB

is a principal
PB
-bundle.
Define

(2.4.23)

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p):=\pi_{\vec{w}}^{\prime\prime}(M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p))\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})

as a locally closed
\mathbb{K}
-subvariety.
By base change (see Corollary
B.11
), the restriction of
\pi_{\vec{w}}^{\prime\prime}

\pi_{\vec{w},p}:M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{a(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)=M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)/PB,

is a principal
PB
-bundle as well as a geometric quotient.

Our first main result improves A. Mellit’s cell decomposition theorem
[
40
, §7]
:

Theorem 2.10
.

If
(C_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},C_{k})\in T^{k}
is very generic (Definition
1.1
and Assumption
1.4
) of type
\bm{\mu}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is nonempty,
then there is a decomposition into locally closed
affine

\mathbb{K}
-subvarieties

(2.4.24)

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=\sqcup_{\vec{w}\in W^{2g}\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}W/W(C_{i})}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})=\sqcup_{\vec{w}\in W^{2g}\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}W/W(C_{i})}\sqcup_{p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta(\vec{w}))}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p),

such that:

(1)

We have

(2.4.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p),\quad\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\times\mathbb{K}^{|U|}\cong\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}.

\displaystyle\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)=a(\vec{w},p)-n+1=|S_{p}|+2gn-2n+2,\quad\overline{b}(\vec{w},p):=b(\vec{w},p)-|U|.

In particular,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is of dimension
\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)+\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)
, and

\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)+2\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)=d_{\bm{\mu}}=n^{2}(2g-2+k)-\text{\tiny$\sum_{i,j}$}(\mu_{j}^{i})^{2}+2

is a constant independent of
(\vec{w},p)
.

(2)

There exists a unique
(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})
such that
\dim\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})
is of maximal dimension
d_{\bm{\mu}}
. Equivalently,
\overline{a}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})=d_{\bm{\mu}}
(resp.
\overline{b}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})=0
).
In particular,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})
is an
open dense algebraic torus
:

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{d_{\bm{\mu}}},\quad\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})=\{\text{pt}\}.

Proof.

(0).
By (
1.2.8
) and Proposition
2.9
, the decomposition of
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is clear.
Next, we show that
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})
is affine. Inspired by (
1.2.7
), we define a closed (affine) subvariety of
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
:

\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w}):=M_{B}^{\prime}\cap(\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{2g}$}B\tau_{j}B\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}B\dot{w}_{i}P_{i}\times\{I_{n}\})\subset M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w}).

Observe that we have mutually inverse
U
-equivariant isomorphisms:

(2.4.26)

\displaystyle U\text{\tiny$\times$}\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})\rightarrow M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w}):(u,(A_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},x_{k-1},I_{n}))\mapsto(uA_{1}u^{-1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},ux_{k-1},u(u^{C_{k}})^{-1});

\displaystyle M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})\rightarrow U\text{\tiny$\times$}\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w}):(A_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},x_{k-1},u_{k}=\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{u}^{C_{k}})^{-1})\mapsto(\widetilde{u},(\widetilde{u}^{-1}A_{1}\widetilde{u},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\widetilde{u}^{-1}x_{k-1},I_{n})),

where
\widetilde{u}\in U
is uniquely determined by the equation
u_{k}=\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{u}^{C_{k}})^{-1}
. Thus,
\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})=M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})/U
.
Recall that
U\subset PB_{\mathrm{par}}
is a normal closed subgroup with quotient
PB_{\mathrm{par}}/U\cong PT_{\mathrm{par}}=(T\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}Z(C_{i}))
.
Then by Proposition
B.14
and Proposition
B.9
, we get an isomorphism

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})=M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})/PB_{\mathrm{par}}\cong\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})/(PB_{\mathrm{par}}/U)=\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})/PT_{\mathrm{par}},

and the quotient map
\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})\cong\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})/PT_{\mathrm{par}}
is a principal
PT_{\mathrm{par}}
-bundle as well as a geometric quotient, which is unique up to unique isomorphism. However,
\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
is an affine
\mathbb{K}
-variety and
PT_{\mathrm{par}}
is reductive, we must have

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})\cong\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})/PT_{\mathrm{par}}\cong\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})//PT_{\mathrm{par}}=\mathrm{Spec}\penalty\ \mathcal{O}(\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w}))^{PT_{\mathrm{par}}}.

In particular,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})
is affine, as desired.
It suffices to prove
(1)
and
(2)
.

(1).
We firstly show that
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is affine, the argument is similar to (0).

Recall by (
2.3.1
), we have a
PB_{\mathrm{par}}
-equvariant isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties

\displaystyle(\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{2g}$}B\tau_{j}B)\text{\tiny$\times$}(\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}B\dot{w}_{i}P_{i})\text{\tiny$\times$}U\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle U\text{\tiny$\times$}\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{g}$}(T^{2}\text{\tiny$\times$}(U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{-}\text{\tiny$\times$}U_{\tau_{2j}}^{-}\text{\tiny$\times$}U_{\tau_{2j-1}^{-1}}^{-}\text{\tiny$\times$}U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{-})\text{\tiny$\times$}(U_{\tau_{2j}^{-1}}^{+}\text{\tiny$\times$}U_{\tau_{2j-1}}^{+}))\text{\tiny$\times$}\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{-}\text{\tiny$\times$}U_{\dot{w}_{i}^{-1}}^{-}\text{\tiny$\times$}(U_{\dot{w}_{i}}^{+}\cap N_{i})\text{\tiny$\times$}Z(C_{i})),

\displaystyle((A_{j})_{j=1}^{2g},(x_{i})_{i=1}^{k-1},u_{k})\mapsto(u^{0},(y_{2j-1},y_{2j},\zeta_{2j-1}^{\prime},\zeta_{2j}^{\prime},\zeta_{2j-1},\zeta_{2j},{}^{+}n^{2j},{}^{+}n^{2j-1})_{j=1}^{g},(\xi_{i}^{\prime},\xi_{i},{}^{+}n_{i}^{\prime},z_{i})_{i=1}^{k-1}).

Then,
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})
is a closed affine
PB_{\mathrm{par}}
-subvariety of the latter.
Also,
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w},p):=M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}Z(C_{i})\subset M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})=M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}Z(C_{i})
is a locally closed affine
PB_{\mathrm{par}}
-subvariety.
Define

\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w},p):=M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w},p)\cap(\text{\tiny$\prod_{j=1}^{2g}$}B\tau_{j}B\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}B\dot{w}_{i}P_{i}\times\{I_{n}\})\subset M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})

as a closed subvariety. By the same formula (
2.4.26
), we obtain an
U
-equivariant isomorphism:

U\times\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w},p)\cong M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w},p).

Thus,
M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w},p)/U\cong\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w},p)
is affine. Recall that
\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}Z(C_{i})=(\mathbb{K}^{\times}\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}Z(C_{i}))/\mathbb{K}^{\times}\triangleleft PB_{\mathrm{par}}

with quotient
PB_{\mathrm{par}}/\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}Z(C_{i})=PB
. Similar to (0), by Proposition
B.14
, we have isomorphisms

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)=M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)/PB\cong M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w},p)/PB_{\mathrm{par}}\cong\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w},p)/PT_{\mathrm{par}}\cong\mathrm{Spec}\penalty\ \mathcal{O}(\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w},p))^{PT_{\mathrm{par}}}.

In particular,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is affine, as desired.

Secondly, by definition, the quotient map

M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)=M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)/PB

is a principal
PB
-bundle. Notice that
U\hookrightarrow PB
is a normal closed subgroup with quotient
PB/U\cong PT
.
Then by Proposition
B.14
and Proposition
B.9
,
the quotient map

\mathfrak{p}_{U}:M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{a(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\vec{w},p):=M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)/U

is a principal
U
-bundle, and the quotient map

q_{U}:\mathcal{P}(\vec{w},p)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)=M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)/PB

is a principal
PT
-bundle. By Lemma
B.8
,
q_{U}
is affine, then so is
\mathcal{P}(\vec{w},p)
, as
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is.

Recall that
U
only acts on the factor
\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}
of
M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)
(see also Lemma
2.11
below).
Consider the closed
U
-subvariety
\{1\}\text{\tiny$\times$}\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}\subset M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)
, by Corollary
B.11
and Proposition
B.9
,

\mathfrak{p}_{U}|_{\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}}:\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}\cong\{1\}\text{\tiny$\times$}\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p):=\mathfrak{p}_{U}(\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)})\cong\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}/U\subset\mathcal{P}(\vec{w},p)

is a principal
U
-bundle as well as a geometric quotient. Also,
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\subset\mathcal{P}(\vec{w},p)
is a closed subvariety, hence affine.
Clearly,
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is connected of dimension
\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)=b(\vec{w},p)-|U|
.
Now, by Proposition
B.15
,
\mathfrak{p}_{U}|_{\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}}
is trivial, i.e. we obtain an
U
-equivariant isomorphism

U\times\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\cong\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}.

This proves the second isomorphism in (
2.4.25
).

Thirdly, let’s prove the first isomorphism in (
2.4.25
).
By the uniqueness of geometric quotient, we obtain an isomorphism

\mathcal{P}(\vec{w},p)=(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{a(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)})/U\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{a(\vec{w},p)}\times(\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}/U)=(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{a(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p).

Recall by (
2.4.4
) that
PT
acts freely on the torus factor

\widetilde{T}_{p}(\beta(\vec{w}))\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{a(\vec{w},p)}=\mathbb{K}^{|S_{p}|+2gn-n+1}
of
M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{a(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)}
.
By Lemma
2.11
below, this is equivalent to an injective algebraic group homomorphism

PT\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{n-1}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{a(\vec{w},p)}
.
So, up to a coordinate change, we may assume

(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{a(\vec{w},p)}\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)}\times PT
,
where
\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)=a(\vec{w},p)-n+1
, and
PT
acts via translation on the second factor. Now, we have

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)=\mathcal{P}(\vec{w},p)/PT\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)}\times(PT\times\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p))/PT\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p),

as desired. Here, in the last step:
By Proposition
B.9
, the natural map
(PT\times\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p))/PT\rightarrow PT/PT=\mathrm{Spec}\penalty\ \mathbb{K}
is a fiber bundle with fiber
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
,
hence
(PT\times\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p))/PT\cong\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
.

Finally, it remains to compute
\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)+2\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)
. By Proposition
2.9
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)+2\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)=a(\vec{w},p)-n+1+2b(\vec{w},p)-2|U|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(4g|U|+2\text{\tiny$\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}$}|N_{i}|+2gn-n+1)-n+1-2|U|=(4g-4)|U|+2\text{\tiny$\sum_{i=1}^{k}$}|N_{i}|+2gn-2n+2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2g-2)(n^{2}-n)+\text{\tiny$\sum_{i=1}^{k}$}(n^{2}-\text{\tiny$\sum_{j}$}(\mu_{j}^{i})^{2})+2gn-2n+2=(2g-2+k)n^{2}-\text{\tiny$\sum_{i,j}$}(\mu_{j}^{i})^{2}+2=d_{\bm{\mu}},

which is clearly independent of
\vec{w},p
.
This completes the proof of (1).

(2). By Lemma
1.2
,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
(if nonempty) is connected smooth affine of dimension
d_{\bm{\mu}}
. So, in the decomposition, there exists a unique
(\vec{w},p)

such that
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\subset\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is (open dense) of dimension
d_{\bm{\mu}}
. Moreover, for all
(\vec{w},p)
in the decomposition,

\dim\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)=\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)+\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)\leq\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)+2\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)=d_{\bm{\mu}}.

Thus,
\dim\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)=d_{\bm{\mu}}

\Leftrightarrow

\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)=0

\Leftrightarrow

\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)=0
. The rest follows immediately.
Done.
∎

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
H
be a connected algebraic group and
T^{\prime}=(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{m}_{z_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},z_{m}}
is an algebraic torus, then any algebraic
H
-action on
T^{\prime}
is identical to an algebraic group homomorphism

\rho:H\rightarrow T^{\prime},

where
T^{\prime}
acts on itself by translation. Thus, if
H
is unipotent, then the
H
-action on
T^{\prime}
is trivial.

Proof.

For any
a\in H
, the
H
-action
\rho
on
T^{\prime}
induces an isomorphism

\rho_{a}:T^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\simeq}T^{\prime}:z_{j}\mapsto c_{i}(a)\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{m}$}z_{i}^{a_{ij}},

for some
(a_{ij})\in GL(n,\mathbb{Z})
and
c_{i}(a)\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}
.
As
\rho_{1}=\mathsf{id}
, by continuity,
a_{ij}=\delta_{ij}
. So,
\rho:H\rightarrow T^{\prime}:a\mapsto\rho_{a}=\diag(c_{1}(a),\text{\tiny$\cdots$},c_{m}(a))
becomes an algebraic group morphism. This gives the desired identification.
In addition, if
H
is unipotent, then any
a\in H
is unipotent, hence so is
\rho_{a}\in T^{\prime}
, which is also semisimple. It follows that
\rho_{a}=\mathsf{id}
,
\forall a\in H
. We’re done.
∎

Question
: If
\bm{\mu}
is only generic, does
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
contain an open algebraic torus?

Remark 2.12
.

If
(\vec{w},p)\neq(\vec{w}_{m},p_{m})
in Theorem
2.10
, then
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is smooth affine of dimension

\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)>0
, and
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is
stably isomorphic
to
\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)}
:

\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\times\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\frac{n^{2}-n}{2}}\cong\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)+\frac{n^{2}-n}{2}}
.

This is closely related to the famous
Zariski cancellation problem
(
ZCP
):

If
Y
is an affine
\mathbb{K}
-variety of dimension
d
such that
Y\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\cong\mathbb{A}^{d+1}
, is
Y
always isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{d}
?

The answer is positive if
d=1,2

[
1
,
20
,
44
,
49
]
, and negative for
d\geq 3
in positive characteristic
[
24
,
25
]
; For
d\geq 3
and
\mathrm{char}\penalty\ \mathbb{K}=0
, the problem is still open.

We tend to believe that
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)
is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)}
in general, thus providing a systematical way of constructing counterexamples to
ZCP
for
d\geq 3
and
\mathrm{char}\penalty\ \mathbb{K}=0
.
For example, take
(g,k,n,\bm{\mu})=(0,6,2,((1,1)^{6}))
, by a computation similar to Example
2.13
below, we obtain a cell decomposition of
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
:

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=(\mathbb{K}^{*})^{6}\sqcup((\mathbb{K}^{*})^{4}\times\mathbb{\mathbb{K}})^{\sqcup 12}\sqcup((\mathbb{K}^{*})^{2}\times\mathbb{\mathbb{K}}^{2})^{\sqcup 40}\sqcup\sqcup_{j=1}^{80}\mathcal{A}_{j};\quad\mathcal{A}_{j}\times\mathbb{K}\cong\mathbb{K}^{4},\forall 1\leq j\leq 80.

In other words, the
\mathcal{A}_{j}
’s are 80 potential counterexamples to the 3-dim
ZCP
in characteristic
0
.

2.5.
Examples

We illustrate Theorem
2.10
by two examples.

Example 2.13

(
(g,k,n,\bm{\mu})=(0,4,2,((1^{2}),(1^{2}),(1^{2}),(1^{2})))
: Fricke-Klein cubic)
.

Let
\Sigma_{0,4}:=(\Sigma_{0},\sigma=\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4}\})
be a four-punctured two-sphere,
G=GL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
. So,
T\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{2}
, and
W=S_{2}=\{1,\mathrm{s}_{1}=(1\penalty\ 2)\}
.
Let
\bm{\mu}=((1)^{2},(1)^{2},(1)^{2},(1)^{2})
, so
\bm{\mu}
is very generic (Definition
1.1
, Assumption
1.4
) and

(2.5.1)

C_{i}=\diag(a_{i,1},a_{i,2})\in T,a_{i,1}\neq a_{i,2},\quad 1\leq i\leq 4;\quad\text{\tiny$\prod_{i,j}$}a_{i,j}=1,\quad\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{4}$}a_{i,\psi_{i}(1)}\neq 1,\forall\psi_{i}\in W.

Clearly,
\dim\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=d_{\bm{\mu}}=n^{2}(2g-2+k)-\text{\tiny$\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j}$}(\mu_{j}^{i})^{2}+2=2
.
The example goes back to
[
18
]
.

1)

We firstly compute the cell decomposition of
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
(Theorem
2.10
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=\sqcup_{(\vec{w},p)\in\mathcal{W}^{*}}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p),\quad\mathcal{W}^{*}:=\{(\vec{w},p):\vec{w}\in W^{k-1}=W^{3},p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta(\vec{w}))\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p),\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\times\mathbb{A}^{|U|}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)+|U|},

where
|U|=1
, and

\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)=|S_{p}|+2gn-2n+2=|S_{p}|-2\geq 0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \overline{b}(\vec{w},p)=\frac{1}{2}(d_{\bm{\mu}}-\overline{a}(\vec{w},p))=\frac{1}{2}(4-|S_{p}|)\geq 0.

Thus,
|S_{p}|=2
or
4
. Accordingly,
(\overline{a}(\vec{w},p),\overline{b}(\vec{w},p))=(0,1)
or
(2,0)
.
By the affirmative answer
[
1
,
20
,
44
,
49
]
to the Zariski cancellation problem in
\dim\leq 2
, we have

\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\cong\mathbb{A}^{\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathbb{K}^{\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)}=\mathbb{K}\text{ or }(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{2}.

We would like to compute
\mathcal{W}^{*}
.
For each
\vec{w}=(\dot{w}_{1},\dot{w}_{2},\dot{w}_{3})=(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})\in W^{k-1}=W^{3}=S_{2}^{3}
, denote
\beta:=\beta(\vec{w})
and
\ell:=\ell(\beta)
.
Recall that, for any
p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta)\subset W^{\ell+1}
, denote

J_{p}=\{\underline{\mathrm{s}}_{i_{m}}=p_{m-1}^{-1}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1},m\in S_{p}\}\subset W,

then
p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta)
if and only if the group
\langle J_{p}\rangle
acts transitively on
[2]=\{1,2\}
.

Note
: In this case, it means that
\langle J_{p}\rangle=W
, equivalently,
S_{p}\neq\varnothing
. That is,

\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta)=\{p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta):S_{p}\neq\varnothing\}\subset\mathcal{W}(\beta).

Recall that,
\beta=\beta(\vec{w})=[w_{1}]\circ[w_{1}^{-1}]\circ[w_{2}]\circ[w_{2}^{-1}]\circ[w_{3}]\circ[w_{3}^{-1}]
, so
\ell=\ell(\beta)=2\ell(w_{1})+2\ell(w_{2})+2\ell(w_{3})
.
If
\exists p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta)
such that
S_{p}\neq\varnothing
, then
\ell(\beta)>0
, so
\ell(\beta)\geq 2
, and
w_{i}=\mathrm{s}_{1}=(1\penalty\ 2)
for at least one
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.
Recall that
p
is of the form
(p_{\ell}=\mathsf{id},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},p_{1},p_{0}=\mathsf{id})\in W^{\ell+1}
.
By Definition
1.15
of a walk, there’s no
i
such that
(p_{i+1},p_{i})=(\mathsf{id},\mathsf{id})
(if we could go up, then we must go up).
In particular, we must have
p_{1}=\mathrm{s}_{1},p_{\ell-1}=\mathrm{s}_{1}
. As
S_{p}\neq\varnothing
, we must have
\ell\geq 4
.
This means that
w_{i}=\mathrm{s}_{1}=(1\penalty\ 2)
for at least two
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. We’re left with 4 cases:

(a)

\vec{w}=(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id})\in W^{3}
. Then,
\beta:=\beta(\vec{w})=\sigma_{1}^{4}\in\mathrm{FBr}_{2}^{+}
and
\ell=\ell(\beta)=4
.
Observe that

\mathcal{W}(\beta)=\{(\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id}),p^{1}=(\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id})\}\subset W^{\ell+1}=W^{5}.

Then
\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta)=\{p^{1}\}
, with
S_{p^{1}}=\{2,3\}\subset[\ell]=[4]=\{1,2,3,4\}
,
U_{p^{1}}=\{1\}\subset[4]
, and
D_{p^{1}}=\{4\}\subset[4]
.
Denote
\vec{w}^{1}:=(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id})
. By the previous computation, we have

\overline{a}(\vec{w}^{1},p^{1})=|S_{p^{1}}|-2=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \overline{b}(\vec{w}^{1},p^{1})=\frac{1}{2}(4-|S_{p^{1}}|)=1,\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}^{1},p^{1})\cong\mathbb{K}.

(b)

Similarly, for
\vec{w}^{2}=(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1})\in W^{3}
(resp.
\vec{w}^{3}=(\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1})\in W^{3}
), we have
\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta(\vec{w}^{2}))=\{p^{2}=(\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id})\}
(resp.
\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta(\vec{w}^{3}))=\{p^{3}=(\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id})\}
), and

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}^{2},p^{2})\cong\mathbb{K},\quad\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}^{3},p^{3})\cong\mathbb{K}.

(c)

\vec{w}=(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1})=:\vec{w}^{4}\in W^{3}
. Then,
\beta:=\beta(\vec{w}^{4})=\sigma_{1}^{6}\in\mathrm{FBr}_{2}^{+}
and
\ell(\beta)=6
. Observe that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id}),p^{4}=(\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id}),p^{5}=(\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id}),

\displaystyle p^{6}=(\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id}),p^{7}=(\mathsf{id},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathsf{id})\}\subset W^{\ell+1}=W^{7}.

Thus,
\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta)=\{p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta):S_{p}\neq\varnothing\}=\{p^{j}:4\leq j\leq 7\}
. Denote
\vec{w}^{j}:=(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1}),4\leq j\leq 7
. So,

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}^{j},p^{j})\cong\mathbb{K},\forall 4\leq j\leq 6;\quad\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}^{7},p^{7})\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{2}.

In summary,
\mathcal{W}^{*}=\{(\vec{w}^{j},p^{j}),1\leq j\leq 7\}
, and the cell decomposition of
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
reads:

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=\sqcup_{j=1}^{7}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}^{j},p^{j})=\mathbb{K}^{\sqcup 6}\sqcup(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{2}.

The order on indices is
admissible
(Definition
3.12
). Our example matches with
[
40
, §1.4]
.

2)

Let’s give a concrete description of the variety
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
.
The defining equation of
M_{B}
reads

x_{1}C_{1}x_{1}^{-1}x_{2}C_{2}x_{2}^{-1}x_{3}C_{3}x_{3}^{-1}x_{4}C_{4}x_{4}^{-1}=\mathsf{id},\quad x_{i}\in G.

with an action of
G_{\mathrm{par}}=G\times T^{4}
via conjugation:

h\cdot(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=(h_{0}x_{1}h_{1}^{-1},h_{0}x_{2}h_{2}^{-1},h_{0}x_{3}h_{3}^{-1},h_{0}x_{4}h_{4}^{-1}),\quad h=(h_{0},h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in G\times T^{4}.

Denote

\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}:=M_{B}\cap(G^{3}\times\{I_{2}\})\subset M_{B},

so the defining equation of
\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}
becomes

x_{1}C_{1}x_{1}^{-1}x_{2}C_{2}x_{2}^{-1}x_{3}C_{3}x_{3}^{-1}C_{4}=\mathsf{id},\quad x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in G.

Now by Proposition
B.9
and Proposition
B.12
, we have

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=M_{B}/PG_{\mathrm{par}}\cong\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}/PT_{\mathrm{par}},\quad PT_{\mathrm{par}}=T^{4}/\mathbb{K}^{\times}\hookrightarrow PG_{\mathrm{par}}=(G\times T^{4})/\mathbb{K}^{\times},

where
PT_{\mathrm{par}}\hookrightarrow PG_{\mathrm{par}}:(h_{0},h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\mapsto(h_{0},h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{0})
acts on
\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}
by:

(h_{0},h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\cdot(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}):=(h_{0}x_{1}h_{1}^{-1},h_{0}x_{2}h_{2}^{-1},h_{0}x_{3}h_{3}^{-1}).

Denote
X^{i}:=x_{i}C_{i}x_{i}^{-1}\in G\cdot C_{i}\cong G/T
. Clearly,
G\cdot C_{i}
is affine. Define

\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{X}):=\{(X^{i})_{i=1}^{3}\in\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{3}$}G\cdot C_{i}:X^{1}X^{2}X^{3}C_{4}=\mathsf{id}\},

equipped with the action of
T\ni h_{0}
via conjugation. Then we obtain a cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=1}^{3}G\cdot C_{i}\cong(G/T)^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

By base change,
\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}\rightarrow\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{X})
is a principal
T^{3}
-bundle.
As
T^{3}\hookrightarrow PT_{\mathrm{par}}:(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\mapsto[\mathsf{id},h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}]
is a normal subgroup with quotient
PT\ni[h_{0}]
, by Proposition
B.14
, we have

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}\cong\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}/PT_{\mathrm{par}}\cong(\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}/T^{3})/PT\cong\underline{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{X})/PT.

Clearly, the conjugate action of
h_{0}=\diag(h_{0,1},h_{0,2})\in T
on
X^{i}
is:

h_{0}\cdot X^{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}X_{11}^{i}&h_{0,1}X_{12}^{i}h_{0,2}^{-1}\\
h_{0,2}X_{21}^{i}h_{0,1}^{-1}&X_{22}^{i}\end{array}\right).

Denote

y_{3}:=\mathrm{Tr}(X^{1}X^{2}=(X^{3}C_{4})^{-1}),y_{1}:=\mathrm{Tr}(X^{2}X^{3}=(C_{4}X^{1})^{-1}),y_{2}:=\mathrm{Tr}(C_{4}^{-1}X^{3}C_{4}X^{1}=C_{4}^{-1}(X^{2})^{-1}).

Then a direct computation shows that,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is a (smooth) affine cubic surface defined by

(2.5.2)

\displaystyle\text{\tiny$\sum_{i=1}^{3}$}\det C_{i}y_{i}^{2}+y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}-\det C_{4}^{-1}\text{\tiny$\sum_{\mathrm{cyclic\penalty\ rotation\penalty\ on}\penalty\ 1,2,3}$}(\mathrm{Tr}C_{4}\mathrm{Tr}C_{1}+\mathrm{Tr}C_{2}^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}C_{3}^{-1})y_{1}

\displaystyle+\det C_{4}^{-1}(\text{\tiny$\sum_{i=1}^{4}$}\mathrm{Tr}C_{i}\mathrm{Tr}C_{i}^{-1}+\mathrm{Tr}C_{1}\mathrm{Tr}C_{2}\mathrm{Tr}C_{3}\mathrm{Tr}C_{4}-4)=0.

Note
: when
\det C_{i}=1,\forall 1\leq i\leq 4
, this recovers the Fricke-Klein cubic
[
18
]
,
[
21
, §5]
.

Next, we consider a rank
3
example.

Example 2.14

(
(g,k,n,\bm{\mu})=(0,3,3,((1^{3}),(1^{3}),(1^{3})))
)
.

Let
\Sigma_{0,3}:=(\Sigma_{0},\sigma=\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\})
be the pair of pants,
G=GL_{3}(\mathbb{K})
. So,
W=S_{3}
.
Let
\bm{\mu}=((1^{3}),(1^{3}),(1^{3}))
. So,
\bm{\mu}
is very generic and

C_{i}=\diag(a_{i,1},a_{i,2},a_{i,3})\in T,\penalty\ \penalty\ a_{i,1},a_{i,2},a_{i,3}:\text{pairwise distinct};\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{\tiny$\prod_{i,j}$}a_{i,j}=1,\penalty\ \text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{3}$}a_{i,\psi_{i}(1)}\neq 1,\forall\psi_{i}\in W.

Clearly,

\dim\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=d_{\bm{\mu}}=n^{2}(2g-2+k)-\text{\tiny$\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j}$}(\mu_{j}^{i})^{2}+2=9-3\times 3+2=2
.

By a similar computation, the cell decomposition of
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
reads:

(2.5.3)

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=\sqcup_{j=1}^{9}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}^{j},p^{j})=\mathbb{K}^{\sqcup 8}\sqcup(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{2}.

We have ordered the indices so that we get an admissible total order (Definition
3.12
).

3.
Dual boundary complexes of character varieties

3.1.
Preliminaries on dual boundary complexes

3.1.1.
Setup

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective
\mathbb{K}
-variety.

Definition 3.1
.

A
log compactification
of
X
is a smooth projective variety
\overline{X}
with simple normal crossing (snc) boundary divisor
\partial X=\overline{X}\setminus X
.
Moreover, we say that
\partial X
is
very simple normal crossing
, if every nonempty finite intersection of its irreducible components is
connected
.

By Hironaka’s resolution of singularities, a log compactification
(\overline{X},\partial X)
always exists.
By blowing up further if necessary,
\partial X
will be very simple normal crossing. Say, we’re in this case.

Definition 3.2
.

The
dual boundary complex

\mathbb{D}\partial X
is a simplicial complex such that:

•

Vertices of
\mathbb{D}\partial X
are in one-to-one correspondence with the irreducible components of
\partial X
;

•

k
vertices of
\mathbb{D}\partial X
spans a
(k-1)
-simplex if and only if the corresponding irreducible components have non-empty intersection.

Proposition 3.3

(
[
10
]
)
.

The homotopy type of
\mathbb{D}\partial X
is an invariant of
X
.
i.e. independent of the choice of the log compactification
(\overline{X},\partial X)
.

Example 3.4
.

For any two quasi-projective smooth varieties
X,Y
, take the log compactifications
\overline{X},\overline{Y}
separately, then
\overline{X}\times\overline{Y}
is a log compactification of
X\times Y
with

\partial(X\times Y)=\partial X\times\overline{Y}\cup_{\partial X\times\partial Y}\overline{X}\times\partial Y
.
By a direct calculation, we then have a homotopy equivalence:

(3.1.1)

\mathbb{D}\partial(X\times Y)\sim\mathbb{D}\partial X\star\mathbb{D}\partial Y,

where ‘
\star
’ stands for the
join
of simplicial complexes.
Clearly,
\mathbb{D}\partial\mathbb{K}\sim*
and
\mathbb{D}\partial\mathbb{K}^{*}\sim S^{0}
. Thus,

\mathbb{D}\partial(\mathbb{A}^{1}\times Y)\sim*,

i.e. the dual boundary complex of
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times Y
is contractible. As another example, we have

\mathbb{D}\partial(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{d}\sim S^{0}\star\mathbb{D}\partial(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{d-1}\sim\cdots\sim S^{d-1}.

The cohomology with rational coefficients of a dual boundary complex is computed by:

Proposition 3.5

(See e.g.
[
48
]
)
.

Let
X
be a connected smooth quasi-projective complex variety of dimension
d
, then the reduced rational (co)homology of the dual boundary complex corresponds to one piece of the weight filtration:

\widetilde{H}_{i-1}(\mathbb{D}\partial X,\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathrm{Gr}_{2d}^{W}H^{2d-i}(X(\mathbb{K}),\mathbb{Q}),\quad\widetilde{H}^{i-1}(\mathbb{D}\partial X,\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathrm{Gr}_{0}^{W}H_{c}^{i}(X(\mathbb{K}),\mathbb{Q}).

The latter is equivalent to the former by Poincaré duality.
More recently, the author has provided a motivic generalization, encoding the integral cohomology of the dual boundary complex:

Proposition 3.6

(
[
59
, Prop.0.2]
)
.

For any smooth complex quasi-projective variety
X
, we have

(3.1.2)

H^{i-1}(\mathbb{D}\partial X;\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{\mathrm{W},c}^{i,0}(X;\mathbb{Z}),

where
H_{\mathrm{W},c}^{a,b}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is the
integral singular weight cohomology with compact support
of
X
defined via motives.

3.1.2.
A remove/reduction lemma

In a case, one can simplify the computation of dual boundary complexes:

Lemma 3.7
.

[
55
, Lem.2.3]

Let
X
be a smooth irreducible quasi-projective
\mathbb{K}
-variety, and
Z\subset X
be a smooth irreducible closed subvariety of smaller dimension with complement
U
. If
N_{Z}
is the normal bundle of
Z
in
X
, then we have a natural homotopy cofiber sequence

\mathbb{D}\partial Z\sim\mathbb{D}\partial\mathbb{P}(N_{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{D}\partial X\sim\mathbb{D}\mathrm{Bl}_{Z}(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{D}\partial U.

In particular, if
\mathbb{D}\partial Z\sim\mathrm{pt}
, then the natural map
\mathbb{D}\partial X\rightarrow\mathbb{D}\partial U
is a homotopy equivalence.

By an inductive procedure, one may obtain a further simplification:

Lemma 3.8
.

[
55
, Prop.2.6]

Let
X
be a smooth irreducible quasi-projective
\mathbb{K}
-variety with a non-empty open subset
U
. If the complement
Z=X\setminus U
admits a finite decomposition into locally closed smooth subvarieties
Z_{j}
such that:
\mathbb{D}\partial Z_{j}\sim*
; there is a total order on the indices such that
\cup_{j\leq a}Z_{j}
is closed for all
a
. Then the natural map
\mathbb{D}\partial X\rightarrow\mathbb{D}\partial U
is a homotopy equivalence.

This will be our key tool for computing the dual boundary complex of the character variety
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
.

3.1.3.
A fibration over the dual boundary complex

For completeness, we review “the fibration at infinity” associated to any connected smooth quasi-projective
\mathbb{K}
-variety, say
X
.
For
X=\mathcal{M}_{B}
, such a fibration
\alpha
appeared in the statement of the geometric P=W conjecture
0.1
.

Fix any log compactification
(\overline{X},D=\partial X)
with
D
very simple normal crossing.
Fix a Riemann metric on
\overline{X}
. For
0<\delta\ll 1
, let
N_{\delta}(D)\subset\overline{X}
be the
\delta
-neighborhood of
D
.
Let
D_{i}
,
i\in I=\{1,2,\text{\tiny$\cdots$},m\}
be the irreducible components of
D
.
For each
i
, let
N_{\delta}(D_{i})\subset\overline{X}
be the
\delta
-neighborhood of
D_{i}
, which is a tubular neighborhood of
D_{i}
. Then
\{N_{\delta}(D_{i})\}_{i\in I}
is an open cover of
N_{\delta}(D)
, and

(3.1.3)

\cap_{1\leq j\leq r}N_{\delta}(D_{i_{j}})\neq\varnothing\Leftrightarrow\cap_{1\leq j\leq r}D_{i_{j}}\neq\varnothing.

Now, take any partition of unity
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in I}
associated to this cover. That is,
\rho_{i}\in C^{\infty}(N_{\delta}(D))
s.t.:

(1)

0\leq\rho_{i}\leq 1
and
\sum_{i\in I}\rho_{i}=1
.

(2)

For each
i
, the support
\mathrm{Supp}\rho_{i}
is a compact subset contained in
N_{\delta}(D_{i})
.

Then define a map

\overline{\alpha}=\overline{\alpha}[\{\rho_{i}\}]:N_{\delta}(D)\rightarrow\mathbb{D}D,\quad\overline{\alpha}(x):=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\rho_{i}(x)v_{i},

where
v_{i}
is the
vertex
of
\mathbb{D}D
corresponding to
D_{i}
. By (
3.1.3
), the map is indeed well-defined.

Claim
: the map
\overline{\alpha}
, up to homotopy, is independent of the choice of partition of unity.

Proof of Claim.

Indeed, for any other partition of unity
\{\widetilde{\rho}_{i}\}
, we get a continuous family of partitions of unity
\{\rho_{i}^{t}:=(1-t)\rho_{i}+t\widetilde{\rho}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, parameterized by
t\in[0,1]
. Apply the above definition, we then obtain a continuous family of maps
\overline{\alpha}_{t}=\sum\rho_{i}^{t}v_{i}:N_{\delta}(D)\rightarrow\mathbb{D}D
.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

By the
Claim
and Proposition
3.3
, the map obtained by composition

(3.1.4)

\alpha:N_{\delta}^{*}(D)=N_{\delta}(D)\setminus D\hookrightarrow N_{\delta}(D)\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}}\mathbb{D}\partial X,

is well-defined up to homotopy.

3.2.
Dual boundary complexes of very generic character varieties

Here’s our main result:

Theorem 3.10
.

The weak geometric P=W conjecture
0.2
for very generic character varieties holds:
If
(C_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},C_{k})\in T^{k}
is very generic (Definition
1.1
, Assumption
1.4
) of type
\bm{\mu}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is nonempty, then we have a homotopy equivalence

\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}\sim S^{d_{\bm{\mu}}-1},\quad d_{\bm{\mu}}=\dim\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}.

It relies on the following lemma:

Lemma 3.11

(
[
59
, Cor.0.3]
)
.

If
Y
is a
\mathbb{K}
-variety stably isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{\ell}
,
\ell\geq 1
, then
\mathbb{D}\partial Y
is contractible.

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

By Theorem
2.10
, we get a decomposition into locally closed subvarieties

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{\vec{w}\in W^{2g}\times\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}W/W(C_{i})}$}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{\vec{w}\in W^{2g}\times\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}W/W(C_{i})}$}\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta(\vec{w}))}$}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\cong(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)}\times\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p),\quad\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\times\mathbb{K}^{|U|}\cong\mathbb{K}^{b(\vec{w},p)},

and
\dim\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)=d_{\bm{\mu}}
if and only if
(\vec{w},p)=(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})
, that is,

\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)=d_{\bm{\mu}}
, i.e.,
\overline{b}(\vec{w},p)=\dim\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)=0
.
Thus, for any
(\vec{w},p)\neq(\vec{w}_{\max},p_{\max})
, by (
3.1.1
) and Lemma
3.11
, we have

\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\sim\mathbb{D}\partial(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)}\star\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)\sim\mathbb{D}\partial(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{\overline{a}(\vec{w},p)}\star\mathrm{pt}\sim\mathrm{pt}.

To apply Lemma
3.8
, it remains to produce an
admissible total order
(Definition
3.12
) on

(3.2.1)

\mathcal{W}^{*}:=\{(\vec{w},p):\vec{w}\in W^{2g}\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}W/W(C_{i}),p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta(\vec{w}))\}.

This is done in Corollary
3.15
below. Thus, by Lemma
3.8
, with

X=\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
and
U=\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}_{m},p_{m})=(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{d_{\bm{\mu}}}
, we get a homotopy equivalence

\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}_{m},p_{m})\sim S^{d_{\bm{\mu}}-1}
,
as desired.
∎

To finish the proof of our main theorem, we’re left with the question of admissible total orders.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
X=\sqcup_{i\in I}Z_{i}
be a finite decomposition of a
\mathbb{K}
-variety into locally closed subvarieties.
We say that a total order
\leq
on
I
is
admissible
, if

Z_{\leq a}:=\cup_{i\in I:i\leq a}Z_{i}\subset X

is closed, all
a\in I
. In particular, for the maximal index
m\in I
,
U:=Z_{m}\subset X
is open.

In this case, we call
(X=\sqcup_{i\in I}Z_{i},\leq)
an
admissible decomposition
.

For simplicity, we also denote

I_{\leq a}:=\{i\in I:i\leq a\},\quad I_{<a}:=\{i\in I:i<a\},\quad Z_{<a}:=\cup_{i\in I_{<a}}Z_{i}.

Observe that
I_{<i}=I_{\leq m_{<i}}
, where
m_{<i}
is the maximal element of
I_{<i}
.
It follow by definition that,
Z_{<i}\subset Z_{\leq i}\subset X
are two closed subsets.
Hence, the complement
Z_{i}=Z_{\leq i}\setminus Z_{<i}\subset Z_{\leq i}
is
open
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
(X=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{i\in I}$}Z_{i},\leq)
be an admissible decomposition. Suppose that, for each
i\in I
, we have an admissible decomposition
(Z_{i}=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{j\in J_{i}}$}Z_{i,j},\leq)
. Denote

\widetilde{I}:=\{(i,j):i\in I,j\in I_{i}\}\cong\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{i\in I}$}J_{i}
, and define a total order on
\widetilde{I}
by

(i,j)\leq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\Leftrightarrow i<i^{\prime},\text{ or }i=i^{\prime}\text{ and }j\leq j^{\prime}.

Then
(X=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{(i,j)\in\widetilde{I}}$}Z_{i,j},\leq)
is an admissible decomposition.

Proof.

Clearly, we have a decomposition of
X
into locally closed subvarieties
X=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{(i,j)\in\widetilde{I}}$}Z_{i,j}
.
It suffices to show that the total order
\leq
on
\widetilde{I}
is admissible.
Indeed, we have

Z_{\leq(i,j)}=Z_{<i}\cup(Z_{i})_{\leq j}\hookrightarrow Z_{<i}\cup Z_{i}=Z_{\leq i}\hookrightarrow X.

By assumption,
Z_{\leq i}\subset X
and
(Z_{i})_{\leq j}\subset Z_{i}
are closed. By the observation above, the composition

Z_{\leq i}\setminus Z_{\leq i,j}=Z_{i}\setminus(Z_{i})_{\leq j}\subset Z_{i}\subset Z_{\leq i}

is then open. So, the complement
Z_{\leq i,j}
is closed in
Z_{\leq i}
, hence also closed in
X
. Done.
∎

Next, consider the Bruhat cell decomposition
G=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{\dot{w}\in W/W(P)}$}B\dot{w}P
, where
W(P)
is the Weyl group of a Levi subgroup of
P
.
Recall that there is a Bruhat partial order on
W/W(p)
:
\dot{\lambda}\leq\dot{\mu}
if and only if
B\dot{\lambda}P\subset\overline{B\dot{\mu}P}
.
That is,
\overline{B\dot{\mu}P}=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{\dot{\lambda}\leq\dot{\mu}}$}B\dot{\lambda}P
.
It follows that any total order extending the Bruhat partial order is admissible. From now on, we alway fix such an extension.

Let
\beta\in\mathrm{Br}_{n}^{+}
be a
n
-strand positive braid with a braid presentation
\beta=\sigma_{i_{\ell}}\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\sigma_{i_{1}}
as usual. Recall that the braid variety
X(\beta)
has a
B
-equivariant decomposition (
1.4.10
):

X(\beta)=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta)}$}X_{p}(\beta).

Lemma 3.14
.

There exists a natural admissible total order on
\mathcal{W}(\beta)
.

Proof.

For each
p=(p_{\ell}=\mathsf{id},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},p_{1},p_{0}=\mathsf{id})\in\mathcal{W}(\beta)
, define locally closed subvarieties of
X(\beta)
:

(3.2.2)

X_{(p_{i},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},p_{0})}(\beta)=\cap_{1\leq j\leq i}\overline{f}_{j}^{-1}(Bp_{j}B);\quad\overline{f}_{j}:X(\beta)\rightarrow G:\vec{\epsilon}=(\epsilon_{i})_{i=\ell}^{1}\mapsto\mathrm{B}_{i_{j}}(\epsilon_{j})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1}).

So,
X_{(p_{i},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},p_{0})}(\beta)=\sqcup_{p_{i+1}\in W}X_{(p_{i+1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},p_{0})}(\beta)
.
Then by the Bruhat cell decomposition, have

X_{(\leq p_{i+1},p_{i},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},p_{0})}(\beta)=\cup_{w\leq p_{i+1}}X_{(p_{i},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},p_{0})}(\beta)\cap\overline{f}_{i+1}^{-1}(\overline{BwB}),

hence is closed in
X_{(p_{i},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},p_{0})}(\beta)
.
In other words, the Bruhat total order on

W_{(p_{i},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},p_{0})}:=\{p_{i+1}\in W:X_{(p_{i+1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},p_{0})}(\beta)\neq\varnothing\}\subset W

is admissible. Then by induction, Lemma
3.13
induces an admissible total order
\leq
on
\mathcal{W}(\beta)
.
∎

Finally, as promised in the proof of Theorem
3.10
, we obtain

Corollary 3.15
.

There is a natural admissible total order on the cell decomposition:

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=\sqcup_{(\vec{w},p)\in\mathcal{W}^{*}}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p),

such that
(\vec{w}_{m},p_{m})
is the maximal index.

Proof.

This is more or less a consequence of Lemma
3.14
, and the argument is similar.

Firstly, consider the decomposition

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{\vec{w}\in W^{2g}\times\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}W/W(C_{i})}$}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w}).

Let’s show that it’s
admissible
: there exists an admissible total order on
W^{2g}\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}W/W(C_{i})
.
Equivalently by definition (
1.2.8
) and (
1.2.7
), it means the equivariant decomposition

M_{B}^{\prime}=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{\vec{w}\in W^{2g}\times\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}W/W(C_{i})}$}M_{B}^{\prime}(\vec{w})

is admissible.
Indeed, similar to the proof of Lemma
3.14
, by Lemma
3.13
, we obtain an admissible total order on

W^{2g}\times\text{\tiny$\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}$}W/W(C_{i})
as the compositions of the total Bruhat orders on
G=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{w\in W}$}BwB
and

G=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{\dot{w}_{i}\in W/W(C_{i})}$}B\dot{w}_{i}P_{i}
.

Now, again by Lemma
3.13
, it suffices to show that the decomposition

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w})=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta(\vec{w}))}$}\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}(\vec{w},p)

is admissible. By definition (
2.4.23
), it suffices to show that the equivariant decomposition

M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w})=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta(\vec{w}))}$}M_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\vec{w},p)

in Proposition
2.9
is admissible.
By (
2.3.7
), it amounts to show that the equivariant decomposition

\widetilde{X}(\beta(\vec{w}))=\text{\tiny$\sqcup_{p\in\mathcal{W}^{*}(\beta(\vec{w}))}$}\widetilde{X}_{p}(\beta(\vec{w}))

defined by (
2.3.4
), (
2.4.1
) is admissible.
By definition, this follows from Lemma
3.14
.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

If
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is only generic, a weaker result holds:

\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is a rational homology
(d_{\bm{\mu}}-1)
-sphere.
This can be shown by a completely different argument using the curious hard Lefschetz property
[
40
, Thm.1.5.3]
. For example, see
[
41
, Rmk.7.0.7]
.

3.3.
Further directions

To establish the weak geometric P=W conjecture
0.2
for generic but not necessarily very generic character varieties
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
,
we are left with two open problems:
(I)
.
H^{*}(\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}};\mathbb{Z})
is torsion-free;
(II)
.
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}\partial\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}})=1
.
We give some comments.

Lemma 3.17
.

[
59
, Lem.3.1]
,
Let
X
be a smooth connected affine algebraic variety of dimension
\geq 3
, with any log compactification
(\overline{X},D=\overline{X}-X)
. Then
\mathbb{D}\partial X
is connected and we have a natural surjection

\pi_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(\overline{X})\simeq\pi_{1}(D)\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}\partial X).

Assuming
(I)
, observe that
(II)
is more or less manageable.
If
\dim\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}>2
, by Remark
3.16
,
(I)
, and Lemma
3.17
,

(II)
holds once we know that
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}})
is abelian
, which is expected to be the case.
If
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is very generic, by Theorem
2.10
,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
contains an open dense algebraic torus
(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{d_{\bm{\mu}}}
, which induces a surjection
\pi_{1}((\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{d_{\bm{\mu}}})=\mathbb{Z}^{d_{\bm{\mu}}}\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}})
. This confirms that
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}})
is abelian. If
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is only generic, we expect that
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
still contains an open dense algebraic torus, then the same argument applies.

Alternatively, we consider a case when
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is only generic.
Let
\mathcal{N}(n,c_{1})
be the moduli space of stable rank
n
holomorphic bundles of degree
c_{1}
on a Riemann surface
\Sigma_{g}
of genus
g
.
In
[
15
, Thm.3.1]
, the Yang-Mills functional was used as a Morse-Bott function to show that
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{N}(n,c_{1}))
is abelian, if
n,c_{1}
are coprime and
(g,n)\neq(2,2)
.
In this case,
T^{*}\mathcal{N}(n,c_{1})
is open dense in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}}(n,c_{1})
, the Dolbeault moduli space of stable rank
n
Higgs bundles of degree
c_{1}
on
\Sigma_{g}
.
So, we obtain a surjection
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{N}(n,c_{1}))\simeq\pi_{1}(T^{*}\mathcal{N}(n,c_{1}))\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}}(n,c_{1}))
, and hence
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}}(n,c_{1}))
is also abelian.
Under
\mathrm{NAH}
, we get a diffemorphism
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Dol}}(n,c_{1})\simeq\mathcal{M}_{B}(n,c_{1})
, with
\mathcal{M}_{B}(n,c_{1})=\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
,
k=1,\bm{\mu}=((n))
, and
C_{1}=\exp(-\frac{2\pi ic_{1}}{n})
.
Then,
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{B}(n,c_{1}))
and
\pi_{1}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{B}(n,c_{1}))
are abelian. See also
[
19
, Prop.6.30]
.
We expect that such an argument works more generally.

Now, we speculate on
(I)
. By Proposition
3.6
,
(I)
amounts to part of the weight cohomology with compact support
H_{\mathrm{W},c}^{a,0}(\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}};\mathbb{Z})
being torsion-free. We refer to
[
59
, §1]
for a quick review on weight cohomology with compact support.
Indeed, examples suggest that the following much more general statement should hold for all generic character varieties:

(1)

The weight cohomology with compact support
H_{\mathrm{W},c}^{a,b}(\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}};\mathbb{Z}),\forall a,b\geq 0
is always torsion-free;

(2)

The integral cohomological descent spectral sequence
[
23
, Thm.3]
for
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}

E_{2}^{a,b}=H_{\mathrm{W},c}^{a,b}(X;\mathbb{Z})\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ H_{c}^{a+b}(X(\mathbb{C});\mathbb{Z})

degenerates at
E_{2}
.
In particular,
H_{c}^{j}(\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}};\mathbb{Z})=\oplus_{a+b=j}H_{\mathrm{W},c}^{a,b}(\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}};\mathbb{Z})
is torsion-free.

Notice that such a degeneration fails dramatically for general varieties, it’s then natural to ask that what is the reason behind the degeneration for character varieties.
Due to the motivic nature of
H_{\mathrm{W},c}^{a,b}(X;A)
, we are led to expect that the motives with compact support of generic character varieties take some simple form, in a way compatible with the HLRV conjecture
[
27
, Conj.1.2.1-1.2.2]
.

Finally, we give a remark on a motivic study of character varieties.

Remark 3.18
.

Recall that A. Mellit has established the curious hard Lefschetz (CHL) theorem
[
40
]
for all generic character varieties
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
:

\omega^{m}\cup-:\Gr_{d_{\bm{\mu}}-2m}^{W}H_{c}^{i}(\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}};\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Gr_{d_{\bm{\mu}}+2m}^{W}H_{c}^{i+2m}(\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}:\mathbb{C}),

where
\omega
is the canonical holomorphic symplectic form obtained from quasi-Hamiltonian geometry.
Adapted to our setting, his proof can be divided into two main steps: First, prove CHL for very generic character varieties using the cell decomposition and a gluing property for CHL;
Second, reduce CHL of
generic

\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
to that of very generic character varieties by a
degeneration argument
:

(1)

By varying the monodromy at a virtual puncture,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
is embedded into a central fiber of a singular family of character varieties.
By taking a resolution of the latter,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}
fits into a cartesian diagram (left) which is morally a base change of the cartesian diagram (right)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{sm}}^{=1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{sm}}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{sm}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=X_{\mathrm{sing}}^{=1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{sing}}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{sing}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{X}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where
\pi:\widetilde{G}\rightarrow G=GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is the Grothendieck-Springer resolution and
N
is the subvariety of unipotent matrices.
Moreover,
X_{\mathrm{sm}}^{1}
behaves like a very generic character variety, satisfying CHL with middle weight
d_{\bm{\mu}}+2\dim G/B
;

(2)

The BBDG decomposition theorem
[
3
]
for the Springer resolution
\pi:\widetilde{N}\rightarrow N
implies a natural isomorphism of mixed Hodge complexes of sheaves:

(R\pi_{*}\mathbb{C}_{\widetilde{N}})^{-}\cong i_{*}\mathbb{C}(-\dim G/B)[-2\dim G/B],

where
(-)^{-}
stands for the sign component as a
S_{n}
-representation;

(3)

By base change, there is a natural isomorphism of mixed Hodge complexes of sheaves:

(R\pi_{X*}X_{\mathrm{sm}}^{1})^{-}\cong i_{X*}\mathbb{C}_{X_{\mathrm{sing}}^{=1}}(-\dim G/B)[-2\dim G/B].

Passing to cohomology with compact support, this induces an isomorphism of mixed Hodge structures:

H_{c}^{*}(X_{\mathrm{sm}}^{1};\mathbb{C})^{-}\cong H_{c}^{*}(X_{\mathrm{sing}}^{=1};\mathbb{C})(-\dim G/B)[-2\dim G/B].

(4)

The above isomorphism is compatible with the Lefschetz operator
\omega\cup-
. Then, by the CHL for
X_{\mathrm{sm}}^{1}
,
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\mu}}=X_{\mathrm{sing}}^{=1}
satisfies CHL with middle weight
d_{\bm{\mu}}
.

Given the discussion above, it seems natural to pose the following
question
:

Does the
degenerating argument
for proving CHL for character varieties admit a motivic improvement?

If so, such a motivic result could be used to detect the integral cohomology of the dual boundary complex of generic but not necessarily very generic character varieties.
The latter is the main obstruction for us to prove the full weak geometric P=W conjecture
0.2
.
There’re at least two main challenges in the motivic question:
1. Existence of a motivic lifting/variation of the decomposition theorem for the Springer resolution? For the rational version, see
[
16
]
;
2. The six functor formalism for integral motivic sheaves. For related work, see
[
7
,
56
]
.

Appendix A
Cell decomposition of braid varieties

We’ll prove Proposition
1.16
. We use the notations of Definition
1.14
,
1.15
.
Let
p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta)
,
0\leq m\leq\ell
. Define a closed subvariety of
\mathbb{A}^{m}
and subsets of
[\ell]
:

(A.0.1)

\displaystyle X_{p}^{m}(\beta):=\cap_{1\leq j\leq m}f_{j}^{-1}(Bp_{j}B)\subset\mathbb{A}^{m},\quad X_{p}^{0}(\beta)=\mathrm{pt}.

\displaystyle U_{p}^{m}:=U_{p}\cap[m],\penalty\ \penalty\ S_{p}^{m}:=S_{p}\cap[m],\penalty\ \penalty\ D_{p}^{m}:=D_{p}\cap[m].\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ [m]=U_{p}^{m}\sqcup D_{p}^{m}\sqcup S_{p}^{m}.

Lemma A.1
.

We have
p(X(\beta))\subset\mathcal{W}(\beta)
. Moreover, for any
p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta)
and
1\leq m\leq\ell
,

\displaystyle X_{p}^{m}(\beta)\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{K}_{\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}}\times X_{p}^{m-1}(\beta)&\text{if $p_{m}=\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}>p_{m-1}$\hskip 10.22217pt(go-up)};\\
X_{p}^{m-1}(\beta)&\text{if $p_{m}=\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}<p_{m-1}$\hskip 10.22217pt(go-down)};\\
\mathbb{K}_{\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}}^{\times}\times X_{p}^{m-1}(\beta)&\text{if $\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}<p_{m-1}$ and $p_{m}=p_{m-1}$ \hskip 10.22217pt(stay)}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

For any
\vec{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{A}^{\ell}
, denote
p:=p(\vec{\epsilon})\in W^{\ell+1}
.

(1)

If
\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}>p_{m-1}
, that is,
\ell(\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1})=\ell(p_{m-1})+1
. By Proposition
1.10
, we have

[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})]=[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m-1}}(\epsilon_{m-1})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})]\in\mathrm{BD}_{n};\Rightarrow p_{m}=\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}\penalty\ (\text{go-up}).

Let’s define a parameter
\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{K}
:
By the unique decomposition

Bp_{m-1}B=Bp_{m-1}U_{p_{m-1}}^{-}:\mathrm{B}_{i_{m-1}}(\epsilon_{m-1})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})=b_{m-1}(\epsilon_{m-1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\epsilon_{1})p_{m-1}L_{p_{m-1}}^{-}(\epsilon_{m-1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\epsilon_{1}),

we get
b_{m-1}\in B
. Then
\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{K}
and
b_{m}\in B
are uniquely determined by the equation

(A.0.3)

[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})]\circ[b_{m-1}]=[b_{m}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

Or, by Proposition
1.10
:

[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})]=[b_{m}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})]\circ[p_{m-1}]\circ[L_{p_{m-1}}^{-}]\in\mathrm{BD}_{n}
.
In fact,

(A.0.4)

\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}=(b_{m-1})_{i_{m},i_{m}}^{-1}(b_{m-1})_{i_{m}+1,i_{m}+1}\epsilon_{m}+(b_{m-1})_{i_{m},i_{m}}^{-1}(b_{m-1})_{i_{m},i_{m}+1}.

This shows that
X_{p}^{m}(\beta)\cong\mathbb{K}_{\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}}\times X_{p}^{m-1}(\beta)
.

(2)

If
p_{m-1}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}<p_{m-1}
. So,
\ell(p_{m-1}=\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}^{\prime})=\ell(p_{m-1}^{\prime})+1
. By Proposition
1.10
,

B\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}^{\prime}B=U_{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}^{-1}}^{-}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}Bp_{m-1}^{\prime}U_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}:\mathrm{B}_{i_{m-1}}(\epsilon_{i_{m-1}})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})=\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(c_{m-1})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}b_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}.

So,
[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})]=[\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}+c_{m-1})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}b_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}]\in\mathrm{BD}_{n}
. Define
\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{K}
by

(A.0.5)

B=TU:b_{m-1}^{\prime}=D_{m-1}^{\prime}u_{m-1}^{\prime};\quad\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}+c_{m-1})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}D_{m-1}^{\prime}=D_{m-1}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}.

More concretely, write
D_{m-1}^{\prime}=\diag((D_{m-1}^{\prime})_{1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},(D_{m-1}^{\prime})_{n})\in T
, then we have

(A.0.6)

\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}=(D_{m-1}^{\prime})_{i_{m}+1}^{-1}(\epsilon_{m}+c_{m-1})(D_{m-1}^{\prime})_{i_{m}}.

By the Bruhat cell decomposition for
GL(2,\mathbb{K})
, we have:

(A.0.7)

\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}I_{n}&\text{\tiny{$\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}=0$}};\\
a_{m}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}d_{m}^{\prime}\text{ \tiny$\in B\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}U_{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}^{-}$},&\text{\tiny{$\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\neq 0$}}.\end{array}\right.\penalty\ \text{\tiny$a_{m}^{\prime}=\mathrm{K}_{i_{m}}(-\epsilon_{m}^{\prime-1})\mathrm{K}_{i_{m}+1}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(-\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}),\penalty\ d_{m}^{\prime}=\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime-1})$},

according to the following computation:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})^{-1}&0\\
0&\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})^{-1}\\
0&1\end{array}\right).

(2.a)

If
\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}=0
, then

[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})]=[b_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}]\Rightarrow p_{m}=p_{m-1}^{\prime}=\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}\penalty\ (\text{go-down}).

This shows that
X_{p}^{m}(\beta)\cong X_{p}^{m-1}(\beta)
.

(2.b)

If
\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\neq 0
, then by Proposition
1.10
, we have:

[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})]=[D_{m-1}^{\prime}a_{m}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}d_{m}^{\prime}u_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}]=[D_{m-1}^{\prime}a_{m}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}d_{m}^{\prime}]\circ[u_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}]\in\mathrm{BD}_{n},

and
\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})\in B\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}^{\prime}B
. So,
p_{m}=p_{m-1}
(stay), and
X_{p}^{m}(\beta)\cong\mathbb{K}_{\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}}^{\times}\times X_{p}^{m-1}(\beta)
.

Now, if
\vec{\epsilon}\in X(\beta)
, then
p_{0}=p_{\ell}=\mathsf{id}
by definition. So,
p\in\mathcal{W}(\beta)
. Done.
∎

Now, we fulfill our promise:

Proof of Proposition
1.16
.

(0).
The action of
b\in B
on
\vec{\epsilon}\in X(\beta)
is uniquely determined by

[\widetilde{b}_{m}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{m})]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{1})]=[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})]\circ\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})]\circ[b^{-1}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.\penalty\ (\forall 1\leq m\leq\ell)

Apply
g_{-}
(Definition
1.6
), we see
X_{p}(\beta)
is
B
-invariant. The rest follows from Lemma
A.1
.

(1).
By above,
\widetilde{b}_{m}\in B
and
\widehat{\epsilon}_{m}\in\mathbb{K}
are determined inductively by

(A.0.9)

[\widetilde{b}_{m}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{m})]=[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})]\circ[\widetilde{b}_{m-1}]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

By diagram calculus (Lemma
1.7
),
\widetilde{b}_{m}\in TU_{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}^{+}
, and by induction,
\widetilde{b}_{m}\in U_{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}^{+}
if
b=u\in U
.

(1.a). If
b=u\in U
, so
\widetilde{b}_{m}=\widetilde{u}_{m}\in U_{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}^{+}
. Again by diagram calculus, have

U_{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}^{+}X=XU_{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}^{+},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall X\in T,X=\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}},\text{ or }X=\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(c).

In particular, we can define
\widetilde{u}_{m}^{\prime}\in U
by

\widetilde{u}_{m}^{-1}D_{m-1}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}=D_{m-1}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\widetilde{u}_{m}^{\prime-1}.

If
m\in S_{p}
, so
p_{m}=p_{m-1}=s_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}^{\prime}
and
\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\neq 0
. By the decomposition (
1.3.7
), we compute

\displaystyle[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{m})\cdots\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{1})]=[\widetilde{u}_{m}^{-1}\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\cdots\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})u^{-1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\widetilde{u}_{m}^{-1}D_{m-1}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}u_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}u^{-1}]\quad(\text{By $(2.b)$ in the proof of Lemma \ref{lem:inductive_cells_for_braid_varieties}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[D_{m-1}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\widetilde{u}_{m}^{\prime-1}u_{m-1}^{\prime}(p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{+}(L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}u^{-1})p_{m-1}^{\prime-1})p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}(L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}u^{-1})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\widehat{D}_{m-1}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\widehat{u}_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}\widehat{L}_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}]\in\mathrm{BD}_{n},

where the last equality gives:
\widehat{L}_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}\in U_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}
,
\widehat{u}_{m-1}^{\prime}\in U
,
\widehat{D}_{m-1}^{\prime}=D_{m-1}^{\prime}
, and
\widehat{\epsilon}_{m}^{\prime}=\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}
, as desired.

(1.b).
By diagram calculus (Lemma
1.7
),
for any
\lambda=\diag(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in T
, we have

[\mathrm{B}_{j}(\epsilon)]\circ[\lambda^{-1}]=[\mathrm{s}_{j}\mathrm{H}_{j}(\epsilon)]\circ[\lambda^{-1}]=[(\lambda^{\mathrm{s}_{j}})^{-1}]\circ[\mathrm{s}_{j}\mathrm{H}_{j}(\lambda_{j}\lambda_{j+1}^{-1}\epsilon)]=[(\lambda^{\mathrm{s}_{j}})^{-1}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{j}(\lambda_{j}\lambda_{j+1}^{-1}\epsilon)].

Then by (
A.0.9
) and induction, we obtain

\widehat{\epsilon}_{m}=(t^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m-1}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}}})_{i_{m}}(t^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m-1}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}}})_{i_{m}+1}^{-1}\epsilon_{m}=t_{(\mathrm{s}_{i_{m-1}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}})^{-1}(i_{m})}t_{(\mathrm{s}_{i_{m-1}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}})^{-1}(i_{m}+1)}^{-1}\epsilon_{m},\quad\widetilde{b}_{m}=(t^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}}})^{-1}.

(1.b.1).
If
m\in U_{p}
, then
p_{m}=\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}
. By (1) in the proof of Lemma
A.1
,

\widehat{b}_{m-1}p_{m-1}\widehat{L}_{p_{m-1}}^{-}=\mathrm{B}_{i_{m-1}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{m-1})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{1})=\widetilde{b}_{m-1}^{-1}\mathrm{B}_{i_{m-1}}(\epsilon_{m-1})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})t^{-1}=\widetilde{b}_{m-1}^{-1}b_{m-1}p_{m-1}L_{p_{m-1}}^{-}t^{-1}.

Then a simple computation gives
\widehat{b}_{m-1}=t^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m-1}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}}}b_{m-1}(t^{p_{m-1}})^{-1}
,
\widehat{L}_{p_{m-1}}^{-}=tL_{p_{m-1}}^{-}t^{-1}
. So,

\displaystyle[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{m})]\circ[\widehat{b}_{m-1}]=[t^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}}}\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})(t^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m-1}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}}})^{-1}]\circ[t^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m-1}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}}}b_{m-1}(t^{p_{m-1}})^{-1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[t^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}}}]\circ[b_{m}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})]\circ[(t^{p_{m-1}})^{-1}]=[\widehat{b}_{m}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{m}^{\prime})]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

This implies that

\widehat{\epsilon}_{m}^{\prime}=(t^{p_{m-1}})_{i_{m}}(t^{p_{m-1}})_{i_{m}+1}^{-1}\epsilon_{m}^{\prime},\quad\widehat{b}_{m}=t^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}}}b_{m}(t^{p_{m}})^{-1}.

(1.b.2).
If
m\in S_{p}
, so
p_{m}=p_{m-1}=s_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}^{\prime}
and
\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\neq 0
. By
(2.b)
in the proof of Lemma
A.1
,

\displaystyle[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{1})]=[\widetilde{b}_{m}^{-1}\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})t^{-1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\widetilde{b}_{m}^{-1}D_{m-1}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}u_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}t^{-1}]=[\widehat{D}_{m-1}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\widehat{\epsilon}_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\widehat{u}_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}\widehat{L}_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}]\in\mathrm{BD}_{n}.

Then a simple computation gives

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{m-1}^{\prime}=t^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\cdots\mathrm{s}_{i_{1}}}D_{m-1}^{\prime}(t^{-1})^{p_{m-1}^{\prime}},\quad\widehat{\epsilon}_{m}^{\prime}=(t^{p_{m-1}})_{i_{m}}(t^{p_{m-1}})_{i_{m}+1}^{-1}\epsilon_{m}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{u}_{m-1}^{\prime}=t^{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}u_{m-1}^{\prime}(t^{p_{m-1}^{\prime}})^{-1},\quad\widehat{L}_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}=tL_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}t^{-1}.

Altogether, we have proved (1).

(2). By the decomposition

Bp_{m}B=Bp_{m}U_{p_{m}}^{-}:\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})=b_{m}p_{m}L_{p_{m}}^{-}
, we define

(A.0.10)

\mathfrak{\mu mon}_{m}:X_{p}^{m}(\beta)\rightarrow T:(\epsilon_{m},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\epsilon_{1})\mapsto D(b_{m})\in T.

In particular,
\mathfrak{\mu mon}=\mathfrak{\mu mon}_{\ell}
, and
\mathfrak{\mu mon}_{0}=I_{n}
. We will prove by induction.

Case 1. If
\ell(\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1})=\ell(p_{m-1})+1
, i.e.
m\in U_{p}
and
p_{m}=\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}
. By
(2)
in Lemma
A.1
,

\mathrm{B}_{i_{m-1}}(\epsilon_{m-1})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})=b_{m-1}p_{m-1}L_{p_{m-1}}^{-};\quad[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})]\circ[b_{m-1}]=[b_{m}]\circ[\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})]\in\underline{\mathrm{FBD}}_{n}.

If follows that

(A.0.11)

\mathfrak{\mu mon}_{m}(\epsilon_{m},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\epsilon_{1})=D(b_{m})=D(b_{m-1})^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}=(\mathfrak{\mu mon}_{m-1}(\epsilon_{m-1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\epsilon_{1}))^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}\in T.

Case 2.
If
p_{m-1}=s_{i_{m}}p_{m-1}^{\prime}
with
\ell(p_{m-1})=\ell(p_{m-1}^{\prime})+1
. We use
(2)
in Lemma
A.1
. So,

\mathrm{B}_{i_{m-1}}(\epsilon_{m-1})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})=\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(c_{m-1})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}b_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}=b_{m-1}p_{m-1}L_{p_{m-1}}^{-}.

Observe that

D(b_{m-1})=(D(b_{m-1}^{\prime}))^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}=(D_{m-1}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}\in T
.
Again, by
(2)
in Lemma
A.1
,

\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})=D_{m-1}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}\mathrm{H}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}u_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}.

Case 2.1.
If
m\in D_{p}
, then
p_{m}=p_{m-1}^{\prime}
and
\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}=0
. Thus,

\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})=b_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}=b_{m}p_{m}L_{p_{m}}^{-},

with
b_{m}=b_{m-1}^{\prime}
and
L_{p_{m}}^{-}=L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}
. By above, it follows that

(A.0.12)

\mu_{m}(\epsilon_{m},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\epsilon_{1})=D(b_{m})=(D(b_{m-1}))^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}=(\mathfrak{\mu mon}_{m-1}(\epsilon_{m-1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\epsilon_{1}))^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}\in T.

Case 2.2.
If
m\in S_{p}
, then
p_{m}=p_{m-1}
and
\epsilon_{m}^{\prime}\neq 0
. By
(2.b)
in Lemma
A.1
,

\mathrm{B}_{i_{m}}(\epsilon_{m})\text{\tiny$\cdots$}\mathrm{B}_{i_{1}}(\epsilon_{1})=D_{m-1}^{\prime}a_{m}^{\prime}\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}d_{m}^{\prime}u_{m-1}^{\prime}p_{m-1}^{\prime}L_{p_{m-1}^{\prime}}^{-}=b_{m}p_{m}L_{p_{m}}^{-},

where
a_{m}^{\prime},d_{m}^{\prime}
are given by (
A.0.7
).
So
D(b_{m})=D(a_{m}^{\prime})D_{m-1}^{\prime}=D(a_{m}^{\prime})D(b_{m-1})^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}
. That is,

(A.0.13)

\mu_{m}(\epsilon_{m},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\epsilon_{1})=\mathrm{K}_{i_{m}}(-\epsilon_{m}^{\prime-1})\mathrm{K}_{i_{m}+1}(\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})\mu_{m-1}(\epsilon_{m-1},\text{\tiny$\cdots$},\epsilon_{1})^{\mathrm{s}_{i_{m}}}\in T.

Now, (
1.4.12
) follows by induction from (
A.0.11
), (
A.0.12
), and (
A.0.13
).
This proves (2).
∎

Appendix B
Quotients of varieties

We collect some results on quotients of varieties. Hopefully, it helps to digest the main body of this article.
Most statements below are standard, so we skip the proofs whenever possible.

Recall our
Convention

1
:
A
\mathbb{K}
-variety
means a
reduced separated scheme of finite type
over
\mathbb{K}
.

Convention

7
: Fix
G,H
as linear algebraic groups over
\mathbb{K}
, unless otherwise stated.

We refer to
[
30
]
for the background on various quotients for algebraic group actions. Occasionally, the notation
X/^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{ge}}}G
is used for geometric quotient.

B.1.
Principal bundles

A principal
G
-bundle means so in the étale topology unless stated otherwise.

Proposition B.1
.

If
G\curvearrowright X

freely
, and
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is a
flat
orbit map into a
\mathbb{K}
-variety
, then:

(1)

The fiber product
X\times_{Y}X
is
G
-isomorphic to
X\times G
, i.e. we get a cartesian diagram:

(B.1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x,g)\mapsto(x,xg)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(2)

\pi:X\rightarrow Y
