is smooth and affine.

(3)

\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is a principal
G
-bundle (in the étale topology).

Remark B.2
.

In above,
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is in fact a geometric quotient. See Proposition
B.9
.

Proof of Proposition
B.1
.

(1). The proof is covered by the three commutative diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Delta}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\times\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{Y}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\circ p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Delta}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\times\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{Y}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

First, consider the left cartesian diagram. As
Y
is
separated
,
\Delta_{Y}
is a closed embedding, so is
\widetilde{\Delta}_{Y}:X\times_{Y}X\rightarrow X\times X
by base change.
A free action means the morphism
a:X\times G\rightarrow X\times X:(x,g)\mapsto(x,xg)
is a closed embedding.

\pi
is
G
-invariant means
\pi\circ p_{1}=\pi\circ p_{1}\circ a=\pi\circ p_{2}\circ a:X\times G\rightarrow Y
.

This induces the middle commutative diagram.
As
a
and
\widetilde{\Delta}_{Y}
are closed embeddings, so is
c:X\times G\rightarrow X\times_{Y}X:(x,g)\mapsto(x,xg)
, by the
cancellation property
for closed embeddings.

Now, consider the right commutative diagram. By assumption, each closed fiber of
\pi
is isomorphic to
G
. Then so is
p_{1}:X\times_{Y}X\rightarrow X
by base change. Thus,
c
is an isomorphism on the closed fibers over
X
, hence a dominant morphism.

By base change and composition,
X\times_{Y}X
is of finite type over
\mathbb{K}
, and hence
p_{1}:X\times_{Y}X\rightarrow X
is a surjective
flat
morphism of schemes
of finite type
over
\mathbb{K}
, with
reduced
closed fibers and
reduced
base
X
. Then,
X\times_{Y}X
is reduced by Lemma
B.3
below.
Now,
c
is a a closed embedding and a dominant morphism between reduced schemes, hence an isomorphism. This proves (1).

(2). Clearly,
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is
fpqc
.
By
[
57
,
Lemma 02VL
]
(resp.
[
57
,
Lemma 02L5
]
), a morphism being smooth (resp. affine) is fpqc local on the target. Then by the cartesian diagram in (1),
\pi
is smooth and affine, as
p_{1}:X\times G\rightarrow X
is.

(3). By (2),
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is a
smooth covering
. By
[
57
,
Lemma 055V
]
(
slicing smooth morphisms and refining a smooth covering by an étale covering
), there exists a morphism
s:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow X
such that the composition
\pi\circ s:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow X\rightarrow Y
is an étale covering. Now, by (1), the base change of
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
along
\pi\circ s:\mathcal{V}\xrightarrow{s}X\xrightarrow{\pi}Y
is
G
-isomorphic to
\mathcal{V}\times G
over
\mathcal{V}
.
This gives a local trivialization of
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
in the étale topology. This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma B.3

(Reducedness)
.

If
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a surjective morphism of schemes
of finite type
over any field
k
, and all
closed
fibers (i.e.
X_{y}
,
\forall
closed point
y\in Y
) of
f
are
reduced
, then

(1)

Any fiber
X_{y}
of
f
is reduced (the point
y\in Y
may not be closed).

(2)

If in addition
Y
is reduced and
f
is flat, then
X
is also reduced.

Remark B.4

(Jacobson schemes)
.

Let
Y
be a scheme of finite type over any field
k
.

(1)

Recall by
[
57
,
Definition 02J1
]
that, a point
y\in Y
is a
finite type point
if the canonical morphism
\mathrm{Spec}\penalty\ k(y)\rightarrow Y
is of finite type. Equivalently by
[
57
,
Lemma 01TA
]
,
y
is a closed point in some affine open subset
U=\mathrm{Spec}\penalty\ R
of
Y
, and the field extension
k\hookrightarrow k(y)
is finite.

(2)

By
[
57
,
Lemma 02J6
]
,
Y
is
Jacobson
: the closed points are dense in every closed subset
[
57
,
Definition 01P2
]
. Equivalently, every nonempty locally closed subset contains a closed point.

(3)

Now by
[
57
,
Lemma 01TB
]
, the closed points in
Y
are precisely the finite type points.

In particular, if
k
is an algebraically closed field, then the closed points of
Y
are the
k
-points, and every nonempty locally closed subset contains a closed (i.e.
k
-) point.

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

(1). Take any point
y\in Y
, define
Y^{\prime}:=\overline{\{y\}}\hookrightarrow Y
equipped with the reduced close subscheme structure. Then
Y
is integral and
y
is a generic point of
Y^{\prime}
. Let
f^{\prime}:X^{\prime}=X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
be the base change of
f
along
Y^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y
, which is still a surjective morphism of schemes of finite type over
k
.
By base change and our assumption, all the closed fibers of
f^{\prime}
are also reduced. Moreover,
X_{y}^{\prime}=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(y)=X_{y}
.

If
X_{y}^{\prime}
is non-reduced, then by
[
57
,
Lemma 0575
]
, there exists a nonempty open subset
V\subset Y^{\prime}
such that, for all
v\in V
the fiber
X_{v}^{\prime}
is non-reduced.
However, by Remark
B.4
,
Y
is Jacobson
3
3

3

It’s this step that the finite type assumption in the lemma becomes essential.
, hence
V
contains a closed point. This is a contradiction.

(2). This follows from (1) and
[
22
, Cor.3.3.5]
(alternatively, see
[
38
, Thm.23.9, Cor.]
):

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a flat morphism between two locally Noetherian schemes.
If
Y
is reduced at the
points
of
f(X)
, and
f^{-1}(y)
is a reduced
k(y)
-scheme,
\forall
point
y\in f(X)
, then
X
is reduced.

∎

Remark B.5
.

As in the proof of Proposition
B.1
(3), a similar argument also shows:

If
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is a morphism of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties and
F
is a
\mathbb{K}
-variety, then
\pi
is a fiber bundle with fiber
F
in the étale topology if and only if
\pi
is a fiber bundle with fiber
F
in the smooth topology.

The flatness condition in Proposition
B.1
can be relaxed when the base is normal:

Proposition B.6
.

If
G
acts
freely
on a
pure dimensional
variety
X
over
\mathbb{K}
(of char.
0
), and
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is an orbit map, with
Y
a
normal

pure dimensional

\mathbb{K}
-variety, then:

(1)

\pi
is flat. So Proposition
B.1
applies:
\pi
is smooth, affine, and a principal
G
-bundle, etc.

(2)

X
is normal.

Proof.

(1). By Lemma
B.7
,
\pi
is flat, so Proposition
B.1
applies.

(2). This follows from (1) and
[
57
,
Lemma 034F
]
:
a morphism being normal is local in the smooth topology
: If
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
is a smooth morphism, then
S
is normal
\Rightarrow
so is
S^{\prime}
. If
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
is smooth surjective, then
S^{\prime}
is normal
\Rightarrow
so is
S
.
∎

Lemma B.7

(
A variant of miracle flatness
.
[
50
, Thm.3.3.27]
)
.

If
R\rightarrow S
be a local morphism of Noetherian local rings,
R
is an excellent normal local domain with perfect residue field, and the closed fiber is regular of dimension
\dim S-\dim R
,
then
R\rightarrow S
is faithfully flat.

As a moral converse to Proposition
B.1
, we have

Lemma B.8
.

If
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is a principal
G
-bundle in
fpqc topology
, with
X,Y

\mathbb{K}
-varieties, then:

(1)

\pi
is smooth and affine.

(2)

We have a natural
G
-isomorphism
c:X\times G\xrightarrow{\simeq}X\times_{Y}X:(x,g)\mapsto(x,xg)
, i.e. the cartesian diagram (
B.1.1
) holds. In addition, the
G
-action on
X
is free.

(3)

\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is an orbit map and a principal
G
-bundle in the étale topology.

Proof.

(1). The proof is similar to that of Proposition
B.1
(2).

(2). As in the proof of Proposition
B.1
(1), we get a
G
-morphism
c
in a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

and a closed embedding
X\times_{Y}X\hookrightarrow X\times X
(
Y
is
separated
). It remains to show
c
is an isomorphism.

By our assumption and base change,
p_{1}:X\times_{Y}X\rightarrow X
is a principal
G
-bundle in the fpqc topology. So, there exists a fpqc covering
\tau:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow X
and a
G
-isomorphism
\phi_{\tau}:\mathcal{V}\times_{Y}X\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{V}\times G
over
\mathcal{V}
.
In other words, we obtain the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\times G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\times G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau\times\mathsf{id}_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{c}_{\tau}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

As a
G
-morphism over
\mathcal{V}
,
\widetilde{c}_{\tau}:=\phi_{\tau}\circ c_{\tau}:\mathcal{V}\times G\rightarrow\mathcal{V}\times G
is then a
G
-isomorphism over
\mathcal{V}
.
Thus, so is
c_{\tau}
.
Now, by
[
57
,
Lemma 02L4
]
,
a morphism being an isomorphism is fpqc local on the target
. It follows that
c:X\times G\rightarrow X\times_{Y}X
is a
G
-isomorphism over
X
. We’re done.

(3). As
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is smooth surjective, by base change, the closed fibers of
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
are the same as those of
p_{1}:X\times_{Y}X\rightarrow X
, which are isomorphic to
G
by (2).
Thus,
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is an orbit map.
Now, the result follows from Proposition
B.1
(3).
∎

B.2.
Associated fiber bundles

A useful reference is
[
35
]
.
Recall
Convention

B
.
As promised in Remark
B.2
, we complement Proposition
B.1
, Proposition
B.6
, and Lemma
B.8
.

Proposition B.9

(Associated fiber bundles)
.

Let
F
be an
affine

H
-variety over
\mathbb{K}
, and
\mathfrak{p}:P\rightarrow B
be an (étale) principal
H
-bundle
4
4

4

By Proposition
B.1
, it suffices to assume:
\mathfrak{p}
is a flat orbit map of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties for a free action
H\curvearrowright P
.
.

H
acts on
P\times F
diagonally:
h\cdot(a,z):=(ah^{-1},hz)
. Then:

(1)

The action
H\curvearrowright P\times F
admits a
geometric quotient

\pi:P\times F\rightarrow P\times^{H}F=(P\times F)/H
,
with
P\times^{H}F
is a
\mathbb{K}
-variety.
In particular,
\mathfrak{p}:P\rightarrow B
is a geometric quotient, with
F=\mathrm{Spec}\penalty\ k
.

(2)

The canonical map
\pi:P\times F\rightarrow P\times^{H}F
is a principal
H
-bundle.

(3)

The induced map
q:P\times^{H}F\rightarrow B:[a,z]\mapsto\mathfrak{p}(a)
is an (étale)
fiber bundle with fiber
F
.

(4)

We have a fiber product diagram
l

(B.2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\times F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\times^{H}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Here,
p_{i}
always stands for the projection to the
i
-th factor.

Proof.

This is similar to
[
35
, Lem.3.4.1]
.
∎

Example B.10

(Homogeneous spaces.
[
5
, II.Thm.6.8]
)
.

Let
H\subset G
be a closed subgroup. Then
q:G\rightarrow G/H=G/^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{ge}}}H
is a principal
H
-bundle over a smooth quasi-projective
\mathbb{K}
-variety.

B.3.
Functorial properties and applications

Finally, we give some applications.

Corollary B.11

(base change)
.

If
\mathfrak{p}:P\rightarrow B
is a principal
H
-bundle, with
P^{\prime}\subset P
a locally closed
H
-subvariety, then
B^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{p}(P^{\prime})\subset B
is a locally closed subvariety, and we get a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{p}|_{P^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}}

In particular,
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}:P^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime}
is a principal
H
-bundle as well as a geometric quotient.

Proof.

By the composition
P^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\overline{P}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow P
, it suffices to consider the case when
P^{\prime}\subset P
is an open or a closed
H
-subvariety.
By Proposition
B.9
,
\mathfrak{p}:P\rightarrow B
is a geometric quotient. Then as an
H
-invariant subset,

P^{\prime}\subset P
is open (resp. closed) if and only if
B^{\prime}\subset B
is open (resp. closed), and
P^{\prime}=\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(B^{\prime})
.
It remains to show that the obviously commutative diagram is cartesian.

The open case is trivial. For the closed case, take
P^{\prime\prime}:=B^{\prime}\times_{B}P
and
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathfrak{p}|_{P^{\prime\prime}}:P^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime}
in the category of schemes. By base change,
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}
is a principal
H
-bundle. In particular,
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}
is flat, and its closed fibers are isomorphic to
H
, hence reduced. Of course,
B^{\prime}
is reduced, then so is
P^{\prime\prime}
by Lemma
B.3
. On the closed subset
P^{\prime}=\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(B^{\prime})
, there is a unique reduced close subscheme structure. Thus, the natural morphism
P^{\prime}\rightarrow P^{\prime\prime}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Proposition B.12

(Reduction of principal bundles)
.

Let
H\subset G
be a closed subgroup,
X
be a
G
-variety, and
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed
H
-subvariety. So
H\curvearrowright G\times Z:h\cdot(g,z)=(gh^{-1},h\cdot z)
.
If:

(1)

\pi:X\rightarrow B
be a principal
G
-bundle over a
\mathbb{K}
-variety
B
.

(2)

\widetilde{a}:G\times Z\rightarrow X:(g,z)\mapsto gz
is a principal
H
-bundle.

Then
\mathfrak{r}:=\pi\circ i:Z\rightarrow B
is a principal
H
-bundle, and we have a cartesian diagram:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad(\Rightarrow Z/H=Z/^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{ge}}}H\xrightarrow{\simeq}X/G=X/^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{ge}}}G\xrightarrow{\simeq}B).

Proof.

Clearly,
\mathfrak{r}:Z\rightarrow B
is an
H
-invariant morphism. We have the following cartesian diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{B}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{G}\times i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,x)\mapsto(gx,x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Here,
p_{i}
denotes the projection to the
i
-th factor.
a:G\times X\rightarrow X:(g,x)\mapsto gx
is the action map. So,
\widetilde{a}=a\circ(\mathsf{id}_{G}\times i)
. The isomorphism
G\times X\xrightarrow{\simeq}X\times_{B}X
follows from Lemma
B.8
. Then by assumption and base change,
\mathfrak{r}:Z\rightarrow B
is a principal
H
-bundle in the smooth topology, hence in the étale topology by Lemma
B.8
. It remains to show (
B.3.1
): the right square is cartesian by above; By Lemma
B.8
, so is the outer square. The rest is due to Proposition
B.9
.
∎

Remark B.13
.

In above, by Propositions
B.1
,
B.6
, the conditions (1)-(2) can be relaxed to:

•

The
G
-action on
X
is free.

•

\pi:X\rightarrow B
and
\widetilde{a}:G\times Z\rightarrow X
are either
flat
orbit maps, or orbit maps between
pure dimensional
varieties with
B

normal
.

Proposition B.14

(Quotient of a principal bundle by a subgroup)
.

Let
H\subset G
be a closed subgroup such that
G/H
is
affine
. Let
\mathfrak{p}:P\rightarrow B=P/G
be a principal
G
-bundle, then:

(1)

There exists a geometric quotient
\mathfrak{p}_{H}:P\rightarrow P/H
, and
\mathfrak{p}_{H}
is a principal
H
-bundle.

(2)

The natural map
q_{H}:P/H\rightarrow B
is a fiber bundle with fiber
G/H
in the étale topology.

(3)

If in addition,
H\triangleleft G
is a normal subgroup, then
q_{H}:P/H\rightarrow B
is a principal
G/H
-bundle.

Proof.

Let
F:=G/H
and
X:=P\times F
. So,
G
acts diagonally on
X
:
g\cdot(p,zH):=(pg^{-1},gzH)
.
As
F=G/H
is
affine
, by Proposition
B.9
, we conclude that
G\curvearrowright X
admits a geometric quotient

\pi:X=P\times F\rightarrow Y:=P\times^{G}F
,
which is a principal
G
-bundle, and the induced map
q_{H}=q:Y=P\times^{G}F\rightarrow B
is a fiber bundle with fiber
F
in the étale topology. Moreover, we obtain the following cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\times G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X=P\times G/H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P=P\times^{G}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y=P\times^{G}G/H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{P}\times\overline{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p,g)\mapsto(p,gH)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p,g)\mapsto pg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\mapsto[p,H]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[p,gH]\mapsto\mathfrak{p}(p)}

(1). By the left cartesian square,
\mathfrak{p}_{H}:P\rightarrow Y=P\times^{G}G/H
is a principal
H
-bundle.
By Proposition
B.9
(1),
\mathfrak{p}_{H}
is also a geometric quotient. So we can write
P/H=Y=P\times^{G}G/H
.

(2).
By the right cartesian square above,
q_{H}:P/H=Y\rightarrow B
is a fiber bundle with fiber
G/H
in the smooth topology, hence in the étale topology by Remark
B.5
.

(3).
The action map
a:P\times G\rightarrow P
induces an action

P/H\times G/H\rightarrow P/H:(pH,gH)\mapsto pHgH=pgH
.
Then by the right cartesian square above
q_{H}:Y=P/H\rightarrow B
is a principal
G/H
-bundle in the smooth topology, hence in the étale topology by Lemma
B.8
.
∎

Proposition B.15
.

Let
G
be a
unipotent
algebraic group and
B
be an
affine
variety over
\mathbb{K}
. Then any principal
G
-bundle
\mathfrak{p}:P\rightarrow B
is trivial.

Note
:
G
is connected:
\pi_{0}(G)=G/G^{0}
is a finite unipotent algebraic group, which must be trivial.

Proof.

First, assume
G
is commutative.
Then,
G=G_{a}^{m}
for some
m\geq 0
, as its (abelian) Lie algebra is a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space with trivial Lie bracket.
Now, the principal
G
-bundles over
B
are classified by
H_{\mathrm{\text{\'{e}}t}}^{1}(B,G_{a}^{m})
(see
[
57
,
Lemma 03F7
]
). By
[
57
,
Proposition 03DW
]
and the affine vanishing property,
H_{\mathrm{\text{\'{e}}t}}^{1}(B,G_{a}^{m})\cong H^{1}(B,\mathcal{O}_{B}^{m})=0
. Thus,
\mathfrak{p}
is a trivial principal
G
-bundle.

In general, we prove by induction on
\dim G
. Say,
n:=\dim G>0
.
Let
H:=Z(G)
be the center of
G
. Then,
H
is a commutative unipotent algebraic group of positive dimension, and
\overline{G}:=G/H
is a unipotent algebraic group of dimension
<n
.
By Proposition
B.14
, there exists a
\mathbb{K}
-variety
\overline{P}:=P/H
such that,

\mathfrak{p}_{H}:P\rightarrow\overline{P}
is a principal
H
-bundle, and
q_{H}:\overline{P}\rightarrow B
is a principal
\overline{G}
-bundle.
By induction,
q_{H}
is trivial, hence admits a section
s:B\rightarrow\overline{P}
. We then obtain the following commutative diagram in which the square is cartesian:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{-1}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{H}}

By base change,
\widetilde{p}_{H}:s^{-1}P\rightarrow B
is a principal
H
-bundle. As
H
is a commutative unipotent algebraic group,
\widetilde{p}_{H}
is trivial by above, hence admits a section
s^{\prime}:B\rightarrow s^{-1}P
. Then,
\widetilde{s}\circ s^{\prime}:B\rightarrow P
defines a section of
\mathfrak{p}:P\rightarrow B
. This implies that
\mathfrak{p}
is a trivial principal
G
-bundle.
∎
